Ireland’s labour market steadies but AI hiring is up, finds report

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Indeed’s research finds that the country’s labour market is consistent, while unemployment rates are low. Recruitment platform Indeed has published the findings of its latest ‘Labour Market Snapshot’, which explores how the Irish employment space is performing in the third quarter of the year. The research finds that Ireland’s labour market has steadied over the course of the last few months, with new job postings comparable to an early 2020, pre-pandemic baseline. Ireland’s labour market remains close to full employment, with the unemployment rate remaining at 5pc and the employment rate close to highs. According to Indeed’s report, this is in line with a labour market that has normalised following a period of persistently elevated labour demand post-Covid. “Our latest data suggests Ireland’s labour market has moved into a more normalised phase following the hiring demand we have seen after the pandemic,” said Jack Kennedy, a senior economist at Indeed. The report finds that the majority of sectors tracked sit below their pre-pandemic levels in terms of overall postings on Indeed, with the remaining 41pc sitting at or above baseline. Postings for pharmacy, as well as cleaning and sanitation, are furthest above the February 2020 baseline, while listings for IT infrastructure, operations and support, and childcare are furthest below pre-pandemic levels. Kennedy added, “While overall demand has stabilised, the picture varies by sector, location and occupation, with AI increasingly becoming an important factor of Ireland’s hiring landscape.” Indeed’s research also found that the rate at which AI is being adopted in Ireland is increasing. Almost 15pc of all Irish postings to the jobs site were found to include AI-based terminology in the description, the highest among all European countries tracked by Indeed. Corresponding rates elsewhere included 9.7pc in the UK, 5.6pc in Germany, 5pc in Italy and 3.6pc  in the Netherlands. Further afield, it was 6.7pc in the US. “AI is certainly changing the skills employers are looking for and Ireland is at the forefront of that shift. Around 15pc of Irish job postings mention AI, one of the highest shares internationally,” said Kennedy. “We’re increasingly seeing AI create demand for new skills and roles, and that demand is spreading well beyond traditional tech jobs. The challenge is making sure people, particularly those early into their careers, have the opportunity to develop the skills employers will need as the world of work changes.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Silicon Republic
Maynooth researchers build advanced DNA computer
2026-09-18

Maynooth researchers build advanced DNA computer

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
From left: Janet Adio, Prof Damien Woods and Dr Abeer Eshra. Image: Maynooth University The research points to new possibilities for long-term data storage, energy-efficient computation and molecular systems, according to the team. A team of Maynooth University researchers has developed a “thermodynamically favoured molecular computer” that is thought to be one of the most complex and fastest DNA computers yet created. The work of Prof Damien Woods, Dr Abeer Eshra, Dr Constantine Evans, Janet Adio and Dr Tristan Stérin was published this week (16 September) in the journal Nature. Their research demonstrates a molecular computer made from strands of DNA that can perform multiplication, division and addition. Unlike conventional computers that run on silicon chips, the system is a collection of interacting DNA strands in water without a continuous electricity supply that needs only a small amount of heat to kick-start computations. Woods said: “Silicon-based computers use so much energy; 23pc of Ireland’s electricity goes into computing and data storage. We’ve been blinkered by only seeing one type of computer, but there are other examples around us, including our brain.” According to the team, the research points to new possibilities for long-term data storage, energy-efficient computation and molecular systems that could operate inside cells for applications such as disease detection. The team created the DNA computer by adding a small drop of water and salt to a test tube, together with short pieces of DNA and a longer DNA scaffold. This ‘recipe’ was then heated and cooled, allowing the DNA to perform a computation. “The molecules interact, form a structure and that structure is the answer. One key innovation is that the system naturally finds that answer without needing continuous energy inputs,” said Woods. The university said its DNA computer was “shown to be robust and reusable” and was capable of performing up to 25 different calculations in a row. “A small droplet of liquid contains billions, and sometimes trillions, of DNA strands. These strands interact with one another to produce a result,” said Eshra. “The reaction happens fast in the test tube, but not as fast as silicon – nor is it intended to be. But compared to other DNA computers, ours is the fastest.” Woods leads an EU-funded DNA computing initiative at the university’s Hamilton Institute that explores alternative forms of computing. He said: “This is blue-skies science. We don’t know where the future is going to take us.” The team’s Nature-published research can be viewed here. Silicon Republic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Bolt will own and operate the new fleet of autonomous vehicles. Estonian ride-hailing service Bolt has partnered with US carmaker Lucid to deploy at least 25,000 fully autonomous vehicles (AVs) across multiple European cities, marking the latest efforts by businesses to bring robotaxis to the region while regulators attempt to play catch-up with the technology. The partnership will utilise Bolt’s extensive European data along with Lucid’s software-defined vehicle platform to develop an autonomous driving system (ADS)-ready vehicle platform designed for level-four autonomous mobility. The platform will be built on Nvidia’s Hyperion, a production-ready AV reference architecture. Bolt will own and operate the new vehicles, it said. The company hopes to grow its AV fleet to 100,000 by 2035. The Estonian company will work across product development through to commercial operation in its new partnership, helping define vehicle requirements as well as software, safety and rider-experience parameters. It will also build the fleet infrastructure, operating systems and city partnerships needed to deploy the AVs at scale. “Autonomous driving in Europe requires data, software, vehicles and operations to work as one system built for European roads and regulation,” said Markus Villig, the founder and CEO of Bolt. “Lucid brings a world-class platform, and together, we will co-design the vehicle and software based on our data and more than a decade of operating experience across more than 850 cities.” Robotaxis are a common sight across major US and Chinese cities, but expanding operations to older European cities with often rainy weather has been a challenge for global AV leaders. Last month, Alphabet-owned Waymo announced it would introduce driverless robotaxis on German roads by the end of next year, while Uber and Pony AI said they would deploy more than 2,000 robotaxis across Europe. Uber launched its first European robotaxi service in Croatia late last month in partnership with Pony and Croatian mobility company Verne, while its London deployment began taking rides earlier this month. So Many Women Are Miscarrying in Detention Centers That ICE Has Lost Count

Futurism | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Immigration and Customs Enforcement facilities are horrifying for anybody, embodied by deplorable living conditions, torturous treatment, and a sickening lack of quality healthcare. Unfortunately, a record number of pregnant women have been swept up and sent to such facilities — and it’s hard to imagine a worse place to be during the prenatal period. Horrifying new reporting by the Guardian took a hard look at the state of health care for pregnant women and teenagers held in ICE custody. During the first nine months of Trump’s return to the White House, the agency recorded 18 miscarriages from women held in its custody, an already appalling statistic. Yet as the Guardian found, ICE’s miscarriage records only go up to October of 2025, and any subsequent loss of pregnancy has been lost in the shuffle. That blatant disregard for critical health information underscores the nightmarish conditions pregnant women face in federal detention facilities. One woman, an asylum seeker from Nicaragua named Anabell, relayed her experience to the Guardian. After being detained at the end of a routine government check-in, Anabell says she was given two pregnancy tests, both of which came back positive. That was it, however — the standard blood test and dosage of prenatal vitamins were nowhere to be found, and the amount of protein she was given hardly changed. A month into her detention, Anabell told the Guardian she started to bleed. “I told them I need to see a doctor,” she said, though ICE officers were unmoved. After spending another day in the ICE facility, Anabell’s fellow detainees finally convinced guards to bring her to an emergency room, where she was shackled to a bed and monitored by three ICE agents. Through an interpreter, Anabell learned that she had lost the pregnancy. She has continued to face health complications including bleeding, vomiting, rashes, as well as hair and weight loss, for which she hasn’t received any additional care. “I don’t know what is going to happen to me,” she told the paper. As Sarah Decker, a senior attorney at the Robert and Ethel Kennedy Human Rights Center told the Guardian, it’s unlikely ICE is truly unaware of the amount of pregnant women in the department’s custody, or the horrible health conditions they face. “Many of the pregnant women that we’ve met at [the South Louisiana processing center in] Basile have told us that they disclosed their pregnancy to ICE officers, that they had to advocate for access to prenatal care and access to a gynecologist, that they needed emergency care for complications, Decker said. “So ICE is clearly aware and I think their desire to kind of obfuscate the access that people have to this data is intended to cover up the level of life-threatening medical neglect that’s happening in these facilities.” More on ICE: ICE Reveals Horrific Plan to Outfit Agents With Electrocution Torture Gloves

It’s clear from reading the profile that Chan does believe vibe coding is a meaningful way of allowing her daughter to “build things,” which she called an “innate” desire that “just came” and — of course — had nothing to do with her father’s obsessive decades-long quest to do just that. “It’s been all on her own,” Chan told Colossus. What this kind of hobby — and unrelenting exposure to AI slop — could do to a developing mind remains a debated subject among experts. But the early consensus is troubling. Some researchers have found that all of that slop is corrupting children’s brains, particularly when it comes to violent and extremist content that more often than not falls through the cracks. And as Maxima Zuckerberg’s father knows all too well, ensuring that underage users aren’t exposed to this type of content is far from easy. Then there’s the risk of having minors becoming socially isolated by turning to AI chatbots for company, a possibility that feels all too real for a young daughter of a tech CEO billionaire. The Majority of Americans Are Now Terrified of AI
2026-09-18

The Majority of Americans Are Now Terrified of AI

Futurism | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
The majority of Americans are harboring some apocalyptic fears about AI. According to a new Politico poll that surveyed over 2,000 adults, about two thirds of the American public believes there’s now at least a “moderate” chance that advanced AI could destroy humanity. Intriguingly, the prevalence of this view extends across political lines, with only slight variation. Americans who voted for Kamala Harris in the 2024 presidential election were 70 percent likely to view AI as at a moderate or higher existential risk, compared to 60 percent to Trump voters. There’s also a large contingent who’re quite fatalistic about the tech. A full 17 percent of Americans overall are “almost certain” that AI could wipe us all out, with an even split between Harris and Trump voters. Meanwhile, less than a quarter — 22 percent — said that AI posed a very small to no risk. The poll, conducted this September, is an intriguing barometer of public sentiment as AI leaders have made alarming claims about the risks posed by their systems’ capabilities. An Anthropic researcher stirred the pot last week when he quit the company and accused it of “gambling with our lives,” claiming that the people building the tech “earnestly believe that it could kill us all.” As alarm brewed, Anthropic’s Dario Amodei penned a lengthy essay calling on the US’s top AI companies to cooperate on slowing down AI development, earning the endorsements of Elon Musk and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman. There’s some real skepticism over the sincerity of the AI industry’s safety claims, but they appear to be a loud minority. Only 26 percent said they thought AI leaders were warning of AI’s existential risks as a marketing stunt, per the poll’s findings. By and large, the public is buying what AI leaders are saying. A hefty 48 percent of Americans agreed that AI is now advanced enough that we should outright pause development. There was more divergence here across party lines, though, with only 44 percent of Trump voters holding this view, compared to 58 percent of Harris voters. Conversely, 28 percent of Harris voters said we should continue building powerful AIs because of their potential benefits, compared to 40 percent of the president’s backers. Americans largely agree that the threat is real, but are split on how to tackle it. More on AI: Fear of AI Destroying Humanity Cause Certain Stocks to Skyrocket

Cops Are Giving Complete Nonsense Reasons to Search Thousands of Flock Cameras
2026-09-18

Cops Are Giving Complete Nonsense Reasons to Search Thousands of Flock Cameras

Futurism | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
The cops who’re using Flock cameras to supposedly track down criminals and stalk their ex-girlfriends alike are quite literally laughing in our faces as they tap into the all-seeing surveillance panopticon. According to a new investigation by the Electric Frontier Foundation, a not insignificant number of police officers are submitting nonsense, joke reasons to officially justify the searches they conduct on Flock systems. One officer with the Lake County, Indiana Sheriff’s Department wrote “LMAO” in the Flock software’s “reason” box, before searching across more than 19,000 cameras in over 1,500 cities and towns. The frivolous reasoning is a case in point of why there’s so much outrage over Flock’s AI-powered cameras — which are nominally intended to be licenese plate scanners — and how they’re frequently being abused by cops. Unlike traditional surveillance cameras, Flock’s are part of a centralized network and require no warrants to use. With such little oversight, it’s easy for police officers to conduct sweeping searches for wanton reasons. In its investigation, which was reported on by 404 Media, the EFF found dozens of examples of cops writing childish responses like “LMAO,” “LOL,” “Hehe,” and “IDK,” in the reason box for Flock searches. An alarming number simply button mashed their keyboards. “ghjhghjg,” was a sufficient reason for one officer in Oklahoma to plow through the recordings of over 83,000 cameras. Thousands of others simply wrote uninformative responses like “investigation” and “test.” The odd cop left short little messages, all seemingly unaware that their stated reasons would become public record. “robbery I don’t remember the case number leave me alone,” one wrote. “f*** this new search engine,” wrote another. Following up on its findings, the EFF contacted police departments  and found that few, if any, of the cops were meaningfully disciplined for their flippant entries. At most, a few were “counseled,” but by and large the departments tried to deflect and offered excuses. Flock, conveniently, changed its software in late 2025 so that cops can no longer type in their own “reason.” Now, they’re provided a dropdown list of pre-made reasons instead.  The EFF blasted the move for causing a “loss of transparency,” as it obviously doesn’t encourage cops to be more serious. It merely papers over the fact that they weren’t treating the powerful surveillance tools seriously in the first place. “This culture of abuse has allowed police to treat a mass surveillance network like their own personal search engine,” condemned the EFF in a post about its findings, “permitting the tracking of the movements of everyday citizens for low-level complaints, personal whims, and sometimes, seemingly, for the lols.” More on privacy: Trump Throws His Weight Behind Flock Cameras

Microsoft Director Privately Admitted AI Was the “Largest Theft of Labor in Human History,” Unsealed Court Documents Show
2026-09-17

Microsoft Director Privately Admitted AI Was the “Largest Theft of Labor in Human History,” Unsealed Court Documents Show

Futurism | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
The threat of perjury has a funny way of forcing people to say the quiet parts out loud. As a recently unsealed and unredacted motion in the ongoing legal battle between the New York Times and OpenAI and Microsoft shows, top AI executives have admitted that training AI models required “an astonishing theft of unprecedented proportions.” In the motion filed on behalf of the NYT, flagged by digital media advocate Jason Kint, attorneys used the quotes of Microsoft director of applied science Brent Hecht and OpenAI cofounder and president Greg Brockman to essentially argue their case for them: that the AI industry built its foundation on an unfathomably large pile of stolen intellectual property. Hecht, for his part, is quoted calling the AI buildout the “largest theft of labor in human history.” It’s a damning admission as the NYT accuses the two AI labs of copying “millions of [the] Plaintiffs’ copyrighted articles in their entirety without permission to produce substitutive commercial AI products.” Brockman, for his part, is quoted saying that “publishers” face an “existential threat” from generative AI, which “are largely substitutive, period, [and] will get more and more substitutive as they get better.” As the plaintiff’s counsel argues, these admissions “eviscerate” any claim to fair use — a statement with which Hecht evidently agrees. “For Defendants to prevail on this defense ‘would,’ the same Microsoft executive recognized, arguably “make a complete mockery of the idea of ‘fair use,'” the motion reveals. The damning documents come during the middle stretch of a case that began in 2023, when the NYT accused the two AI labs not only of vacuuming up its original news content, but of using that content to train AI products meant to compete with traditional news outlets. As Axios recently reported, the battle is far from over, as the presiding judge is expected to decide whether to allow the case to proceed to trial by sometime in 2027. In India, the new iPhone can arrive faster than a pizza
2026-09-18

In India, the new iPhone can arrive faster than a pizza

TechCrunch | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt For some iPhone buyers in India, getting Apple’s brand-new iPhone 18 Pro or iPhone 18 Pro Max did not require lining up outside a store or waiting days for delivery. They needed only a few taps on a quick-commerce app to get one delivered to their doorstep in about 10 minutes. Blinkit, Zepto, Swiggy’s Instamart, and BigBasket — platforms best known in India for delivering groceries and everyday essentials in minutes — began offering the new Pro models as they went on sale in the country on Friday. The phones are, however, also available through Apple’s usual sales channels, including its stores, authorized retailers, and online marketplaces. Putting a new iPhone on a 10-minute delivery app is not entirely new in India. Quick-commerce platforms have offered Apple’s latest devices on launch day previously as well, as they have steadily expanded beyond groceries into categories including electronics, beauty products, and fashion. However, the market behind those deliveries is growing rapidly. India’s quick-commerce sector reached about $9 billion in the first half of 2026, per a recent report by Redseer Strategy Consultants, with the number of monthly users rising to more than 60 million from 8 million three years ago. Google and Deloitte separately estimate the market could reach $50 billion by 2030, with non-food products such as electronics, fashion and beauty accounting for 45% of spending. There were signs of strong demand on launch day. BigBasket said it delivered more than 300 iPhone 18 Pro and Pro Max units in the first hour of sales. Instamart told TechCrunch that Bengaluru led the iPhone 18 Pro-series orders on its platform, followed by Delhi. The Burgundy 256 GB iPhone 18 Pro was Instamart’s most popular variant, accounting for nearly a third of its sales. Nonetheless, within hours of launch, availability of the new iPhones became patchy. TechCrunch found the iPhone 18 Pro models sold out on some quick-commerce platforms and in some locations, while they remained available for delivery elsewhere in cities including Delhi and Bengaluru. Tarun Pathak, research director at Counterpoint Research, said the launch-day deliveries showed that quick commerce could work as a channel for premium smartphones in India’s biggest cities, even though volumes remain small compared with overall iPhone sales. The ability to fulfill high-value smartphone orders provides an “early proof of concept,” Pathak told TechCrunch. “The key question is whether this launch-driven demand can translate into regular smartphone purchases through quick-commerce platforms.” Navkendar Singh, associate vice president at IDC, said India stood out globally for this kind of quick-commerce delivery and was particularly suited to such purchases because consumers in major cities had built trust in the platforms by using them regularly for everyday purchases. Official tie-ups between brands and the platforms also gave consumers greater confidence in buying expensive products through them, he told TechCrunch. For a product like the iPhone, the appeal also comes down to “instant gratification,” Singh said, allowing buyers to avoid queues outside stores or waiting several days for an online order to arrive. Still, he cautioned that quick commerce was not yet a significant sales channel for smartphones. Its role around new iPhone releases is largely a “launch-day marketing phenomenon” that “creates good noise around the launch,” he said. Google’s new ‘CC’ is an AI agent that helps families run their households
2026-09-18

Google’s new ‘CC’ is an AI agent that helps families run their households

TechCrunch | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Google is testing a new product designed to help families coordinate with the support of an AI agent. This week, the search giant introduced a new version of CC, an AI agent designed to work across email, calendar, chats, and tasks. With the update, CC now focuses on keeping families organized, planning for the day ahead, and even handling family-specific tasks, like signing permission slips, creating shopping lists, or crafting weekly meal plans. The move to make CC more family-focused comes as a number of AI startups are experimenting with how to best fit agentic AI into consumers’ lives. Some AI tools, like Ollie and Fambot, for instance, have aimed their services directly at helping parents and families. Google’s CC, meanwhile, began its life as a productivity agent that connected to Gmail, Google Calendar, Google Drive, and the wider web to understand your day, then deliver a “Your Day Ahead” briefing to your inbox. In May, the tool came to the Gemini app as “Daily Brief.” Google said user requests showed that people wanted to use the feature more for household management tasks. They wanted AI to help them keep up with kids’ school schedules, sports practices, bills, meal plans, and more. As a result, Google shifted CC to become an agent for families that helps run their households. Now, CC is getting its own Google account, so it can collaborate with family members on tasks, while also maintaining its own specific set of permissions for accessing data. Google explains that each family member chooses what they want to share — like emails about school events, sports, clubs, birthday party invites, doctor appointments, or anything else. These can be forwarded to CC via its email address, or shared automatically. To automate sharing, users can pick the email senders whose messages they always want to share with CC going forward, like schools, travel companies, clubs, or sports teams. (CC will also suggest email senders to add on a weekly basis.) The company says CC currently supports up to six family members who can share information and collaborate with the agent. Beyond email, CC can also coordinate household activities by tracking important dates and to-dos, and then automatically adding them to the calendar or a shared task list. That means, for instance, every time the orthodontist sends an email confirmation of your next appointment or the school announces a teacher workday, CC can put it on the shared calendar. What’s more, the agent can manage select tasks on a family’s behalf, like filling out permission slips or activity registration PDFs, making school supply shopping lists, planning weekly meals, figuring out drive times between activities, creating shared Docs or Sheets, and more. It will even ask for missing details, as needed, and update its group memory so it can be more helpful going forward. Google notes that CC runs on its own isolated cloud computer, powered by Gemini and Google’s agentic harness, Antigravity. The experiment is only available for U.S. users with a personal Gmail account, who are ages 18 and up. That’s a big drawback for the time being because it means older kids, like tweens and teens, can’t use the service unless they lie about their age on their Google accounts. It also means they can’t use it with their school-provided emails, despite the fact that many attend schools that run everything on Chromebooks and Google apps and have inboxes filled with school-related updates. CC could help larger households, where parents have to coordinate with each other and with other adults, like grandparents, caregivers, nannies, or adult children still living at home, which tends to be more common these days. Existing CC users will be invited via email in the coming days to upgrade their accounts, Google says, while new users can join a waitlist to access the AI agent. Joby Aviation’s 3,100-mile autonomous flight signals its push beyond electric air taxis
2026-09-18

Joby Aviation’s 3,100-mile autonomous flight signals its push beyond electric air taxis

TechCrunch | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt An aircraft equipped with Joby Aviation’s autonomy technology flew more than 3,100 miles across the United States without a human pilot taking control at any point, the company said Friday. The flight not only demonstrated the capabilities of Joby’s autonomy tech, but also showed that the company’s ambitions extend well beyond its pursuit of electric air taxis. Joby said the autonomous aircraft it’s not done yet, and will make its way back to the West Coast with stops in various cities, including Louisville, Kentucky, and Salt Lake City, over the next two weeks. Joby Aviation said the cross-country trip, which it described as the “first-ever autonomous flight across the United States,” included autonomous taxiing, takeoffs, navigation, and landings. The aircraft was remotely supervised from Joby’s headquarters in California and Shaw Air Force Base in South Carolina. A pilot was on board for compliance, but Joby said the aircraft performed the entire operation autonomously. Joby, which used a converted Cessna Caravan aircraft for the trip, said the flight demonstrated that its autonomy technology could handle different environments and missions, including medical transport, disaster response, and defense logistics. It also showcased the potential for a second business for Joby, which is best known for developing electric vertical takeoff and landing (eVTOL) aircraft for an eventual urban air taxi service. Joby has spent years developing and seeking type certification from the Federal Aviation Administration for its all-electric vertical take-off and landing aircraft, which will be used to carry passengers on short trips in urban areas. While Joby’s electric air taxi service gets much of the attention, the company has a longstanding relationship with the U.S. Department of Defense and is increasingly pushing deeper into the military sector. Its autonomy stack, which is designed to be retrofitted onto a wide range of conventional aircraft, is part of that strategy. The company plans to first bring to market a regional airframe equipped with the autonomy stack, which could be used for defense missions as well as commercial freight transport. That effort has been supported by a $17 million Air Force contract with AFWERX. Joby took its most meaningful step into defense in August when it acquired Resonant Sciences for $500 million in cash and stock. Ohio-based Resonant Sciences, which makes radio frequency and sensor systems, is now part of a dedicated defense business within Joby that includes several projects such as the development of turbine-electric and hydrogen-electric aircraft, and an autonomy technology stack. Last year, Joby signed an agreement with defense contractor L3Harris Technologies to “explore opportunities” to develop a new class of aircraft — specifically, a gas-turbine hybrid vertical take-off and landing aircraft that can fly autonomously — for defense applications. The startup was said to be pulling in annual recurring revenue of over $100 million at the time. However, intensifying worries in China over losing AI talent and researchers to the West culminated in Beijing blocking the deal, citing potential violations of export controls and foreign investment rules. Manus has since been untangling itself from the American social media giant, and its early investors and backers have reportedly helped the company buy back its shares at a valuation of about $2 billion. As part of its separation from Meta, the company told its users this August that they would have to export and back up their own data because it had to delete data generated following Meta’s acquisition to “comply with regulatory requirements in specific jurisdictions.” The company this month said it has resumed independent operations, and that its founding team will continue to lead it. Manus makes AI products and agents that are pretty similar to what companies like OpenAI, Lovable and Replit offer. TIME
The Story Behind Netflix's Rousing Bollywood Dance Comedy Best of the Best
2026-09-18

The Story Behind Netflix's Rousing Bollywood Dance Comedy Best of the Best

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Maitreyi Ramakrishnan and Priyanka Kedia in Best of the Best Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Maitreyi Ramakrishnan wants to make this clear: She had to audition for Best of the Best, the coming-of-age comedy playing out against the world of collegiate Bollywood dance competitions. “Please include that Maitreyi Ramakrishan was not just offered this role,” she says, her almighty finger pointing hard at my notebook. “Maitreyi Ramakrishnan worked!” She’s responding, in her beguilingly animated way, to my suggestion that her character, Maya, feels as though she were written for Ramakrishnan, the Never Have I Ever star who was introduced to us in 2020 as the fabulously temperamental teen Devi in Mindy Kaling and Lang Fisher’s Netflix dramedy. Maya was actually conceived around the same time, when Patriot Act host Hasan Minhaj and his writing and producing partner Prashanth Venkataramanujam spent their time in lockdown cooking up Best of the Best, their answer to the Step Up franchise, which is finally hitting Netflix on Sept. 18. For some fans, who were eager to see Devi go to college, Best of the Best might serve as a suitable replacement for a continuation of the show, which ended in 2023 after four seasons. The movie reunites Ramakrishnan with one of her Never Have I Ever directors, Lena Khan, and co-choreographer Joya Kazi, who appeared in its first season, performing a Bollywood-style routine to the song “Nagada Sang Dhol.” In that scene, Kazi fluidly blended India's traditional Kathak dance with the swirling and romantic poses choreographed specifically for the big screen in Mumbai. That fusion, in a nutshell, is what Bollywood dance is all about. Devi, who harbored complicated insecurities about her cultural identity, scoffed at the performance. Ramakrishnan’s Maya, on the other hand, would scoff at anyone challenging her Bollywood-style supremacy. “Maya is that bitch,” says Ramakrishnan. Ramakrishnan and Minjah behind the scenes of the film Courtesy of Netflix We’re at dinner, discussing Ramakrishnan’s leap from leading a TV show to carrying a movie, a conversation we will pick up again a week later on a Zoom call with Minhaj. I meet up with Ramakrishnan at East Tea Can, a restaurant in her Mississauga neighbourhood, west of Toronto. She orders the cardamom black tea for us to share but insists she could finish off the entire pot by herself, and introduces me to mana’eesh, a stone-baked Middle Eastern pizza served with minced beef and lamb. I’ve interviewed Ramakrishnan on several occasions, witnessing how young fans would swarm her in the wild. This time, she avoids all that by strategically choosing a table in the back and facing away from the diners, though our server recognizes her instantaneously, even behind her large burgundy frames. Her nose ring and traditional bell-shaped earrings are less flash and more a reminder that Ramakrishnan, 24, has always been more at home with South Asian culture than Devi was in that first season. That’s one of the many reasons Minhaj felt she was just right to play Maya, the UCLA freshman thrust into competition with her childhood best friend Anjali. The latter is played by Priyanka Kedia, a fresh-faced Indian-American actor originally from the Bay area, who joined Best of the Best after graduating from NYU and catching a breakthrough part in Steven Spielberg’s Disclosure Day. Her Anjali is the more poised social butterfly to the headstrong and rebellious Maya, their lifelong relationship tested when they both make the school’s esteemed Bollywood dance team and must jockey for parts while adjusting to campus life, rigorous academics, and new relationships. Their story draws from Minhaj and co-writer Venkataramanujam’s own college experiences. Kedia and Ramakrishnan Courtesy of Netflix “I'll be honest with you,” says Minhaj later, on that Zoom call. “I for real felt, we would be so lucky to have Maitreyi. She was the person that I felt had the two things that I think Maya needed. Everybody knows that Maitreyi naturally is comedically brilliant. She has that ‘It’ factor that you can't teach. Your Amy Poehlers, your Kristen Wiigs, your Kate McKinnons.” But her comedic talents would also need to be balanced with something deeper. “If you look at Maitreyi’s work, especially in Seasons 3 and 4 of Never Have I Ever, there is an emotional depth, that pathos that she has. I was like, there's no other number one for this film than her.” Despite, Minhaj’s confidence in her, Ramakrishnan auditioned to reassure the whole team she was ready to transition from the snappy sitcom pacing of Never Have I Ever to the more considered emotional stakes of a feature film. Even Kazi, who went to college with Minhaj, had to jump through similar hoops. “She was a dance Superstar at UC Davis,” says Minhaj. “Even when we were interviewing, I had to recuse myself. I needed her LinkedIn profile to speak for itself. The friendship is real. It goes back to the second Bush Administration.” Minhaj is at his parent’s home in Sacramento, dressed in a blue and white baseball jersey. He’s immediately heartened to see my daughter’s bedroom, where I set up shop, in the background, pointing out the kids’ toys scattered on the floor around him. He pulls out framed photos of baby Hasan, clearly taken at some department store photo studio. The space feels familiar, if only because you could immediately imagine it filled with the characters he’s intimately described in his semi-autobiographical standup specials like Homecoming King and The King’s Jester. Minhaj on the set of Best of the Best Courtesy of Netflix Small talk with Minhaj includes some history on the Punjabi community in Northern California, the early 19th century immigrant farmers who would later hide their identity after the Chinese Exclusion Act. “This becomes the milieu of South Asian identity, specifically in Northern California,” says Minhaj, who just unpacked the context behind where his family resides as if he were freestyling a Patriot Act segment for an audience of two. Long before they met, Ramakrishnan was a huge fan of Patriot Act, a Netflix series in which Minhaj wielded punchlines and PowerPoint presentations to unpack politics and the perils of unchecked capitalism. “The first time I met you was way later at one of your first ever performances of Hasan Hates Ronnie / Ronnie Hates Hasan,” she tells Minhaj, referring to his live comedy tour with Ronny Chieng. “And you were in full costume, right? You were dressed as Princess Leia or something like that?” “It wasn’t a costume,” says Maitreyi. You can hear the eyeroll in her voice. “It was fashion.” “But it’s fine. Whatever. Wouldn’t expect you to know, Minhaj.” “It was a karate gi meets Obi-Wan Kenobi up top.” This jabbing rapport reads as a playful generational tete-a-tete between millennial and Gen Z, but it also speaks to the exchange of perspectives that informed their collaboration on Best of the Best. “What you're seeing in the movie,” says Minhaj, “is Maitreyi and Lena coming together, and really masterminding and creating the tone and the feeling of the film.” One of the more traditional dance sequences in Best of the Best Courtesy of Netflix During weekly script sessions, Ramakrishnan would join Khan, Minhaj, and Venkataramanujam to share her input on drafts, and her character. These workshops would come at the end of the day for Ramakrishnan, who had a jam-packed training schedule to ensure she could convincingly play the part of a teen who’s been dancing all her life. Ramakrishnan is the first to admit she’s not a dancer. She spent a year as a child learning Bharatnatyam, the traditional 3,000-year-old dance originating from Tamil Nadu in India, but decided that all that ballet-like discipline wasn’t for her. “The structure of being told, ‘This is what you have to do in this order,’ over and over again, didn't work for my child brain,” says Ramakrishnan. “I just wanted to run around.” She was in her nerves about signing on to do a dance movie and potentially misrepresenting a community that she admires. “Dancers are no joke,” she says. But she says her mother offered the objective perspective she needed to face the job: “You read the script. You don’t need to be a great dancer. You just need to be good at seven dances.” Choreographers Phillip & McKenzie, who tapped Kazi’s cultural expertise, organized a two-month boot camp for Ramakrishnan in L.A. She attended six classes a day, six days a week, training in everything from Bharatnatyam and Kathak to contemporary, hip-hop, heels, and groove. Then it was off to New Jersey, where she joined the rest of the cast and dancers for another two months. That’s four months of training for an eight-week shoot that left Ramakrishan physically broken but with an even deeper appreciation for the commitment and the craft. “I got to witness so many great talents,” she says. “I noticed the dancers that have this sparkle. There’s many that can nail the choreo, be on time with the counts, understand musicality, do the trick and the flip. But then there’s some dancers that can do it all in a way that makes an audience member, who has never danced before in their life, want to get up and dance with them, because you’re as intimidated and impressed by their dancing as you are welcomed and invited.” Maya and Anjali auditioning for the competitive team Courtesy of Netflix That welcoming and invitational vibe can be applied to the movie’s diverse approach to the dances, as well. The term “Bollywood dance” is a bit misleading. Best of the Best boasts a fusion of styles, reflecting trends within Bollywood dance competitions, and the culture online. In the same way that Save The Last Dance folded hip-hop into the ballet space, young classically trained Bharatnatyam dancers in North America have been blending traditional movements with krumping and breaking. The real-life sister dance duo Poonam and Priyanka Shah went viral a decade ago, performing butterflies and splits to the Bollywood song Ramta Jogi, taking their “Bfusion” routines from the collegiate circuit to the internet. In recent years, French-Tamil dancer Usha Jey broke the internet busting moves in a saree to Lil’ Wayne. These children of South Asian immigrants have been using fusion dance to broker a new cultural identity for themselves, with one foot in their ancestral lands and another in the place they call home. Best of the Best is the first popular movie to explore that fusion, and its evolution. There’s storytelling in the dance sequences that shift between Bharatnatyam and Brooklyn styles like bone breaking. That intentionality extends to the soundtrack, which at one point tips its hat to the music that got this cultural exchange started, the early-2000s hip-hop produced by Timbaland and Dr. Dre sampling Bollywood. One extravagant Bollywood musical number features the Truth Hurts song “Addictive,” produced by Dre, battling with the iconic 1969 Hindi song that it famously sampled, “Thoda Resham Lagta Hai.” “The beautiful thing that we wanted to do in this film,” says Minhaj, “is to take all of these things that make our identity and create something new with it.” He turns his attention to the varied identities spread across South Asia. “This is a rare opportunity where all of us from the diaspora can reconcile a lot of paradoxes that we couldn’t necessarily participate in if we were to do this film in India. There are all these other social, political and language barriers that separate Pakistan, Bangladesh, India, and Sri Lanka. They become sequestered to their own cinema. In the States, all of that becomes kind of irrelevant.” Best of the Best breaks up monolithic representations of being South Asian, with a cast hailing from all regions, and a dance troupe composed of cheeky character types. These go beyond the nerd, the heartthrob, the Paul Rudd in Clueless sensitive type and the mean girl, to include the rich slick-haired wealthy “FOBs” (meaning “fresh off the boat”), and even a token white guy (Nico Greetham) so hilariously obsessed with Indian culture that he speaks fluent Hindi. Maya and Anjali bring the moves to a frat party Courtesy of Netflix The filmmakers also accounted for Ramakrishnan, and the very specific Eelam Tamil identity she brings to the film, with all its fraught political connotations. When Maya introduces herself at one point, she says, “I'm Tamil. My family's from Sri Lanka.” The wording is precise. Ramakrishnan’s family is not, for instance, from Tamil Nadu in India, like Devi’s in Never Have I Ever. Nor do they identify as Sri Lankan. The state, which her family fled, stands accused of perpetrating a genocide against the Eelam Tamil population in 2009, after a 25-year civil war. For Ramakrishnan’s family, calling themselves Sri Lankan would be akin to a Palestinian identifying as Israeli. “Is it something that I expect everyone to catch?” asks Ramakrishnan. “No. Everyone will catch that she's saying she's Tamil. She doesn't understand Hindi, because not every South Asian person understands Hindi. But I think the Tamil Eelam folks, like yourself, will catch that.” Ramakrishnan has never shied away from discussing her identity and that history, and how both have shaped her. As a child, she joined her activist parents at protests in Toronto, calling for a ceasefire and an end to the genocide in Sri Lanka. She kept that spirit alive last year, at a “No Tyrants” rally in Vancouver, holding up a sign that said, “Never Have I Ever Thought Genocide Was OK." Ramakrishnan performing a Kuthu dance at a frat party Courtesy of Netflix “Maitreyi has such a strong sense of self, and such a strong sense of pride in her culture,” says Minhaj. He describes one of his favorite moments on set, seeing that cultural pride pour out of her when Ramakrishnan and Kedia’s characters perform a Kuthu dance at a frat party. Kuthu is a high energy, high impact Tamil folk dance that can appear like jumping jacks and aggressive pelvic thrusts colliding. It’s the kind of dance that isn’t taught in professional studios. “A lot of people over time have seen Kuthu as lower-class rowdy street dance,” says Ramakrishnan, She likens Kuthu to Bhangra, which started as a dance for Punjab farmers, and is now a common sight at Indian weddings. The best place to learn both Bhangra and Kuthu? “At the function,” she says. Regarding Bhangra, “You learn it at the wedding. You learn it with the vibes. You get the beat in your shoulders and you're going.” As for Kuthu: “It’s at whatever banquet hall birthday party, for some 2-year-old kid who will never remember any of this, because he’s passed out in the corner while all the uncles and aunties are going off on the dance floor.” The Kuthu sequence is riotous, befitting the chaos of the frat-party environment. Ramakrishnan and Kedia bound over lawn furniture and kick up grass. Ramakrishnan gets all the background actors hyped while she tugs at the collar of her blue checkered shirt, a nod to the red flannel that Prabhu Deva wears during the dance number he wears in the 2007 gangster movie Pokkiri. Maya (Ramakrishnan) in the film's final dance sequence Courtesy of Netflix Minhaj also says that he and Venkataramanujam leaned into Ramakrishnan’s identity for the film’s spectacular finale, which plays out on a stage decorated like a museum, a playground of colonial artifacts. The climactic dance number, according to Minhaj, is meant to inhabit and challenge the South Asian literary discourse on colonial reparations. Did you expect anything less from the duo behind Patriot Act? The finale incorporates the Koh-i-Noor, the 105-carat diamond recorded to be from India that had changed hands across Asian empires for centuries before being surrendered to the British during the Victorian-era. It’s currently set in the Queen Mother’s crown and housed in the Tower of London. Indian politician Shashi Tharoor and British MP Kieth Vaz are among the voices who have called for the diamond’s return. New York City mayor Zohran Mamdani echoed their sentiments earlier this year when pressed about what he would discuss if given the opportunity to meet with King Charles. Minhaj questions whether such a symbolic reparation is useful, especially when it could sow further division in a region torn apart over centuries. Pakistan, Bangladesh, and Afghanistan all lay claim to the diamond of obscure origin, which, as Minhaj points out, could just be an anchor tying a new generation down to a complicated colonial history as opposed to encouraging us to flourish. “Even if the Koh-i-Noor diamond would be returned to us,” he asks, “What does that change for us today? How does it shape our tomorrow? The uncomfortable reality is that it's up to us to shape that, irrespective of that diamond ever coming back to us." I won’t spoil how, but it’s Ramakrishnan, as Maya, who steps up to embody what Minhaj has in mind as the way forward. “Maitreyi is the actor for this moment because she comes from very special cloth. Her parents are incredible activists and academics. She understands her political and social place in history and in time. It only feels right that she's the lead of our film, and the voice of what is new and what is to come.”

How to Make the U.S.-China AI Race Less Dangerous
2026-09-18

How to Make the U.S.-China AI Race Less Dangerous

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The building housing the headquarters of Chinese AI startup DeepSeek in Hangzhou, in China's eastern Zhejiang province on January 14, 2026. The timing could hardly be worse. Even as Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent prepares to meet Chinese Vice Premier He Lifeng this weekend to discuss AI, President Donald Trump has dismissed public warnings about runaway AI as a “hoax.” Meanwhile, an editorial in the state-owned China Daily accused America’s leading AI companies of using calls for a slowdown to “blunt China’s AI advance.” Neither the United States nor China is ready to slow down. Neither trusts the other. And rapidly advancing AI systems will not wait for diplomacy to catch up. But these realities do not mean Trump and Xi can ignore the mounting calls for the U.S. and China to talk about AI. Any such dialogue must avoid the two most common pitfalls for U.S.-China diplomacy: setting the ambitions so low that the talks become meaningless or so high that agreement becomes impossible. Aim too low, and the dialogue becomes a photo op followed by an anodyne communiqué about promoting innovation and reducing risks. Aim too high, chasing sweeping restrictions on frontier development that neither side has the political trust or the capacity to verify and enforce, and the meeting is set up for failure. The former path fails to meet the stakes of the moment; the latter fails to recognize its constraints. A better near-term goal would be for the U.S. and China to pursue AI safety in parallel and demonstrate their progress in practice. That effort should be anchored in technical exchanges that make each country’s AI systems safer on their own terms. The two countries should also establish a protocol to communicate about emerging AI incidents before they spiral into international crises. These steps would represent meaningful progress, far more valuable than lofty statements or unverifiable commitments. The Trump Administration should push for technical exchanges on how to actually test models for dangerous capabilities and behaviors that neither country wants to see released into the wild. These include AI systems capable of helping amateurs develop bioweapons, or evading human oversight and control. American AI companies have been studying these risks for years, and they have also begun to appear in Chinese technical standards related to safety testing. Exchanging best practices and new insights about how to test for and mitigate these risks could render the systems built in both countries meaningfully safer. These proposed technical exchanges would not depend on “trust” or even strict “reciprocity” between the two superpowers, nor would they require identical conceptions of AI risk. They would be driven by self-interest. As the most advanced AI models grow even more powerful, both countries have an interest in ensuring that these systems remain controllable, regardless of where they are built or deployed. That will require both countries to improve their model testing, monitoring and safeguards, and share promising findings that could enhance safety without surrendering a competitive edge or undermining national security. This diplomacy is less like negotiating a sweeping arms-control treaty than establishing shared safety practices and procedures for a technology neither government fully understands or controls. The premise is not that Washington should trust Beijing, or vice versa, but that neither country benefits if the other loses control of a powerful system. For this approach to work, it must be accompanied by a government-to-government crisis-communication protocol. What if an American AI model escapes its company’s control and hacks the networks of a Chinese company or the Chinese government? What if a Chinese AI-powered drone attacks a U.S. Navy vessel, and neither side can immediately determine whether the incident was accidental or deliberate? What if Washington detects a Chinese model conducting a dangerous cyber operation but cannot tell whether Beijing authorized it or whether a company has lost control of it? These are not the kind of existential risks President Trump has dismissed, but they are close cousins of incidents already unfolding in real time. The two countries need to lay the basic groundwork for dealing with them. Whether the communication channel succeeds or fails will depend on its operational design. What constitutes a notifiable incident? Who are the designated contacts? How do the two sides signal that an incident was unauthorized without revealing sensitive intelligence? Serious, technically-informed diplomacy will be required to reach even provisional answers to these questions. One essential feature of the channel should be that information about an incident, at least initially, be transmitted in writing rather than solely by phone. Real-time calls are often poor vehicles for conveying technical information with a Chinese system that runs on committees and documents, increasing the risk of miscommunication and misunderstanding. Sending a message in writing gives the relevant committees in China the ability to disseminate and analyze it and formulate a response that no individual official in China would feel empowered to give. It also reduces the recipient’s ability to shade or distort the message. Even this approach will be challenging, given China’s spotty record of suspending crisis-communication channels—or simply refusing to use them—precisely when tensions are highest. But it would eliminate one of the stumbling blocks: the refusal to pick up the phone. The talks should begin without preconditions or concessions in the broader technology competition. If Beijing seeks to make any progress on AI safety conditional on relief from U.S. export controls on advanced chips and chipmaking equipment, Washington should keep the two issues separate. The goal of AI safety diplomacy is not to turn the United States and China from intense competitors into cooperative partners. It is to keep their competition from producing outcomes neither side wants. The two sides are nowhere near the political conditions necessary for a sweeping agreement. Trump has rejected calls to slow American AI development, while Beijing increasingly interprets American pacing proposals through the lens of technological containment. Neither side seems prepared to accept broad restrictions on frontier development, and the United States has barely begun to regulate its own AI sector, which is a far more important step toward addressing AI risk than what diplomatic engagement can deliver. That is precisely why the two countries should begin with something narrower. Starting modestly does not mean succumbing to fatalism. The politics of AI have shifted dramatically in just a few weeks, and advancing capabilities will continue to alter the calculations in both capitals. The upcoming U.S.-China talks will not be the last word. A limited start could build momentum over time, creating channels, expertise, and habits of interaction that will become more valuable if the trajectory of AI development increases Washington’s and Beijing’s levels of concern about severe risks. Someday, as unlikely as it may seem now, the two countries may realize that they do not have to agree on who should win the AI race to agree that neither side can afford to lose control of it. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

In the Hilarious Youth, Sharon Horgan Nails the Precarity of Middle Age
2026-09-18

In the Hilarious Youth, Sharon Horgan Nails the Precarity of Middle Age

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings There are plenty of shows that could have been titled Youth. Most have casts in their teens and 20s. One of these is called Adults, the joke being that its postcollegiate characters are more like children—playful at best, feckless at worst. HBO’s Youth, a comedy premiering Sept. 20 about a divorced 50-year-old sandwiched between her college-aged son and declining parents, sounds like it might be similarly ironic. It isn’t, really. As in everything its creator and star, Catastrophe and Bad Sisters hitmaker Sharon Horgan, does, the title’s rueful humor conceals an emotionally complex truth. This is a perceptive, spit-takingly funny show about clinging to the most desirable aspects of youth while also craving comforts that can only come with age and experience. If youth is, as the saying goes, wasted on the young, then Horgan’s Alex is a character actually equipped to savor what little she has left of it. “I have 10 years left as a sexual person,” she determines in the premiere. With the petulant son, Ruarí (Aran Murphy, a near-clone of dad Cillian), whom she raised with minimal help from her ex-husband Lorcan (Owen McDonnell) packing up for uni, she’s resolved to end a long celibate streak. This search for not just a sex partner, but also the enduring love that has so far eluded her should be aided by Alex’s genuine youthfulness. But with guys like Lorcan and another former flame, Patrick (a rakish Rupert Friend), dodging adulthood with girlfriends decades their junior, the dating pool seems sparse. Set up on a blind date with a patient 67-year-old (Tim McInnerny’s Michael) who looks like he could be her dad, she can’t hide her disappointment. She treats him atrociously. Then again, the double standard that dictates she’s now too old for men her own age is also pretty atrocious. Sharon Horgan and Aran Murphy in Youth Colin Hutton—HBO At work, meanwhile, Alex is longing to be treated like the accomplished veteran literary agent she is. The trouble is, her boss, Sam (Sharlene Whyte), is also her best friend. They have a great, funny and foulmouthed and in many ways candid, rapport. But the more we see of this perceptively drawn relationship, the clearer it becomes that Sam doesn’t quite consider Alex an equal. In the office, she questions Alex’s judgment. Outside of it, Sam fails to keep her friend’s secrets, with real consequences. It’s a delicate problem for our protagonist to navigate. If Sam were simply toxic, Alex would simply need to realize it and free herself. Instead, Sam is a pragmatic businesswoman with trust issues rooted in her marriage. Which means it may be worth the hard work it would take to save the friendship as well as the professional relationship. No one working in TV is better than Horgan at writing—or playing—selfish and self-sabotaging characters that, like members of our own families, we love all the more for how much they frustrate us. She’s been doing it since her first series, Pulling, a portrait of faintly desperate single women at that age (30ish) when everyone else seems to be marrying, starting families, and getting serious about their careers. Ruarí can be infuriating; he rots in bed watching manosphere videos and treats his mother like a servant. Yet, thanks in large part to Murphy’s sensitive performance, there’s tenderness in him from the beginning. He’s earnestly confused about how to be a man in the world. As well he should be. Sharon Horgan, left, and Sharlene Whyte in Youth Colin Hutton—HBO Horgan is not the kind of TV creator who makes half-hour dramas with the occasional awkward beat and calls them comedies, though. Her shows, even the one about murdering an abusive husband, will always make you laugh out loud, and Youth is no exception. Sometimes it’s pure physical humor; one episode makes an unexpected, perimenopausal period into a running joke (“Apropos of nothing, she’s back”). Sometimes it’s the pleasure of witty banter or a ridiculous sentence that makes perfect sense in context: “If I send you my dad’s number, can you help him turn his IRA podcast off?” (I do wish Horgan had made a bit more space to flesh out Alex's parents, played by Bríd Brennan and David Hargreaves.) Alex and Sam’s work yields a menagerie of delightfully ghoulish literary-world types, from a snakey rival agent who greets them as “sluts” and “lesbians” (Freddie Fox) to a creepy author (Douglas Hodge) who self-published a terrible yet depressingly popular romantasy novel after Alex dropped him as a client (title: The Goblin Seed). In interviews, Horgan has said she sees Youth as a complement to Pulling and Catastrophe, the mid-2010s Prime Video comedy in which her character and a guy who was supposed to be an anonymous hookup (Rob Delaney) decide to build a family around a surprise pregnancy: “There’s a small element of autobiography to it.” The tones are certainly similar, though the range of emotions has broadened with proximity to life’s hard deadlines (fertility, mortality). “I just don’t know if there’s anything left in my life that I like anymore,” Alex admits. She and Sam and Patrick could all be characters from the previous two shows, 10 or 20 years on, with more residual mess than they’d expected to be cleaning up by middle age. Youth suggests that, in fact, disorder springs eternal; if it isn’t a canceled wedding, it’s an elderly parent losing toes to diabetes. Every new stage of life is another form of youth. How exciting. And terrifying. And funny.

Your Shoe Size Can Change as You Age. Here's Why
2026-09-18

Your Shoe Size Can Change as You Age. Here's Why

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings If you’ve always worn the same size shoe but your sneakers and boots feel tighter now that you’re in, say, your 40s or beyond, you’re not alone. People on TikTok are also sharing their experiences with growing feet as they’re getting older. But do your feet really get bigger with age? “Yes, but ‘bigger’ is somewhat misleading,” says Dr. Kimberly Koury, an orthopedic surgeon at Atlantic Health. “Adult feet do not continue to grow in the same way they do during childhood. Instead, the shape of the foot can gradually change with age.” This can happen for a variety of reasons and cause feet to become wider, flatter, and slightly longer, she explains. “Someone may notice that a shoe that fit well when they were younger now feels too tight, even though their actual foot bones have not continued to grow.” Here’s what to know about why your feet may get bigger with age and what to do about it. The main reason is that over time, your arch “flattens and spreads,” says Lesly Robinson, a podiatrist and interim dean of the Temple University School of Podiatric Medicine. This is often due to decreased collagen—the protein that provides strength and elasticity to your skin, muscles, bones, tendons, and ligaments—that naturally happens with age. This can cause the ligaments supporting your arches to become lax, Robinson says. “When the arch falls, the foot both widens and lengthens,” adds Dr. Alexis Dixon, an orthopedic surgeon and foot and ankle specialist in Marina Del Ray, Calif. Another reason feet may change in shape and size is a foot deformity, like a bunion or hammertoe, which become more prevalent as you get older. A bunion is a bump that forms on the inside of your big toe joint, but it’s not an extra bone growth. Instead, it’s a change in alignment, where the big toe moves toward the other toes, Koury says. “That makes the forefoot wider and can make previously comfortable shoes feel too narrow.” This can also contribute to flattening arches, Robinson says. Hammertoes happen when the tissue of your toes weakens, causing the muscles and tendons to buckle and curve, Dixon says. Bunions and hammertoes can be genetic or result from wearing tight, narrow shoes over a lifetime, she says. They’re more common for women. Arthritis is also common in feet and ankles, especially in the big-toe joint, Dixon says. It can cause bone spurs (extra growths of bone tissue) on top of the foot or at the back of the heel, which may rub against your shoe and make it feel too tight. People may notice foot changes starting in their 30s, but there’s no specific age when they happen, says Sindhu Srinivas, a podiatrist in Atlanta. Age-related foot changes are generally more common for women, Koury says. The hormonal fluctuations in pregnancy and menopause affect collagen, and years of wearing high heels or narrow or ill-fitting shoes may contribute to structural changes in the feet. People with previous foot injuries, flat feet or bunions (or a family history of these conditions), and a higher body weight are more at risk, Koury adds. Being overweight puts pressure and stress on the feet, Srinivas says. “Over time, this increased load can contribute to changes in the foot's supporting structures and overall biomechanics.” The changes happen gradually and can affect your foot’s length, width, and arch height, Srinivas says. But how much bigger your feet can get varies. A study published in 2016 examined the foot measurements of 68 women aged 20 to over 80. It found that the widths of the balls of the feet increased by 3 to 4 millimeters per decade, while ball circumferences increased by about 5 to 7 millimeters, high-step circumferences 0.4 to 4.8 millimeters, and heel instep circumferences up to 2 millimeters. “That may sound small, but these changes accumulate over several decades and can become enough to affect shoe fit,” Koury says. Some people may need a wider shoe, while others might need one a half size larger, Dixon says. If your feet seem to be getting wider or longer and you’re experiencing pain, swelling, numbness, tingling, or difficulty walking or doing other activities, see your doctor, Koury says. Also, talk to your doctor if you’re getting recurrent calluses, blisters, or skin breakdown, or if your bunions, hammertoes, or flatfoot are worsening. Sudden foot changes, especially if they only affect one foot, should also get checked, Srinivas says. Pay extra attention to your feet if you have diabetes, neuropathy, or circulation problems, as even minor changes in your shoes’ fit can cause more serious problems, Koury says. Doctors will recommend the best treatment for your feet. This may include orthotics to improve arch support, Robinson says. It’s also possible to modify footwear for bunions or hammertoes, Dixon adds. Physical therapy or exercises may be helpful for some, Koury says. Surgery may be necessary for some foot issues if other treatments don’t help. You can’t necessarily prevent age-related foot changes, but you can take steps to keep your feet healthy as you get older, Koury says. Most important: wear shoes that fit well. Avoid footwear with narrow toe boxes, which compress your toes (and may cause bunions), and limit high heels, which put stress on the front of the foot and may even cause a deformity, Koury says. Shoes should be supportive and have a wider toe box, Robinson says. “It’s helpful to have your feet measured periodically, including the width, not just the length,” Koury says. Maintaining a healthy weight can “help reduce unnecessary stress on the tendons, ligaments, and bones,” Sirinivas says. Regular physical activity strengthens your feet and ankles. Low-impact, weight-bearing activities can maintain foot function and stability, improve balance, and prevent falls, Dixon says. Also try foot exercises, such as standing on one foot while holding onto a counter, or toe scrunches, which involves curling your toes to pick up a washcloth or other object off of the floor. Treat bunions, flattening feet, or other foot problems as early as possible to avoid problems later, Koury says. “The goal is to accommodate the new foot shape and prevent the changes from becoming painful or functionally limiting.”
