How Zoe Kazan Made East of Eden Her Own
2026-09-18

How Zoe Kazan Made East of Eden Her Own

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In June 2024, Zoe Kazan boarded a flight to New Zealand. She was excited. For a mother of small children, a quiet solo flight is a vacation unto itself. She scrolled through the in-flight movies. “They had East of Eden, and I was like, ‘I guess the gods are conspiring,’” she says. She pressed play. Until then she had been avoiding rewatching her grandfather Elia Kazan’s 1955 masterpiece. She was flying halfway across the world to scout locations for her own version of East of Eden, a seven-part adaptation of John Steinbeck’s novel that premieres on Netflix on Oct. 1. She hadn’t wanted the film to influence her. But her script, which she’d begun working on in 2020, was finally turned in. As the end credits rolled, she sighed in relief. “It was less like the book than I remembered,” she says. The film, which introduced a brooding James Dean to cinema, is more inspired by the novel than a direct adaptation of it: It covers only the last third, crucial characters have been excised, and famous lines are missing. “For me,” says Kazan over cold brew, extra ice on the side, at a Brooklyn cafe on an August morning, “the power of the story is how, over 50 years, it shows history repeating itself—or not.” Her faithful adaptation covers the entirety of the multi-generational saga, which reimagines the tale of Cain and Abel twice over. Cyrus (Tracy Letts in the new series), a self-mythologizing Civil War veteran, prefers his gentle son Adam (Christopher Abbott) to the violent one, Charles (Mike Faist). A generation later, Adam’s twin sons Aron and Cal (Joe Anders and Joseph Zada) vie for the affection of their emotionally absent father. Kazan wrangles with the novel’s central question: Whether those boys can escape the shadows of their family history and forge their own paths—and whether America, carrying the sins of slavery, campaigns of violence against Native Americans, and racism against immigrants building a future in the West, can do the same. Both versions are, in Kazan’s estimation, self-portraits of the artists who made them. Renowned for directing A Streetcar Named Desire and On the Waterfront and co-founding the influential Actors Studio from which the school of “method acting” was born, Elia Kazan was called before the House Un-American Activities Committee in 1952. The Hollywood blacklist was in effect and, Faced with the possibility of never being able to work in Hollywood again, he named names. His granddaughter has never read his autobiography, though she has tried. “There’s some very young part of me that’s like, I still want my grandpa to be my grandpa and not have access to all of this adult stuff,” she says. But she believes that Elia Kazan, who died in 2003 when she was 20, saw himself in the black sheep of the family: “He had this cold and demanding father, and I think he was projecting onto Cal.” Made during a period when the director was facing significant backlash, his version focuses on guilt, rejection, and alienation. Writer Zoe Kazan and her star Florence Pugh on the set of Netflix's East of Eden Geoffrey Short—Netflix What most intrigued Zoe Kazan, a new mother when she started on the script, were characters her grandfather rewrote or omitted: Cathy (Florence Pugh), a mother who walks out on her infant sons and whom Kazan calls “irredeemable, irresistible, and so full of agency,” and Lee (Hoon Lee), a Chinese American servant who steps in to raise the boys. Kazan may live closest to Cathy and Lee in her script. But perhaps the reason she was drawn to the project is the notion of living and working in her grandfather’s shadow. One cannot help but draw the parallel between her and the grandchildren in East of Eden, struggling with whether to embrace or break away from a burdensome legacy. “The irony was not lost on me. There’s a literal way that I’m following in my family’s footsteps,” Kazan admits. “I don’t think I’d be doing my job as an artist if I weren’t thinking about my family while making this family story.” Zoe Kazan was a teenager when she fell hard for Steinbeck. At 15, the Los Angeles native embarked on a Steinbeck-themed road trip up the California coast with her parents, dog-eared East of Eden in hand. Years later, she asked her agents to contact her if anyone purchased the rights. The call came in December 2019, while Kazan was shooting in Australia. She emerged bleary-eyed from a flight back to L.A. with her 15-month-old to pitch her idea to the buyers. “I talked about America and the way that we don’t want to look at our shadow, and how the book argues that embracing your shadow, all of yourself, the good and the bad, is the only way to change,” she says. By then she had built a career not just as an actor in The Big Sick and She Said but as a screenwriter with Ruby Sparks, a satirical take on the manic pixie dream girl, and Wildlife, a drama she co-wrote with her partner, Paul Dano. She landed the East of Eden job and spent the next six years in Steinbeck’s world, and much of that time in the head of one of literature’s most complicated female characters, Cathy. Cathy appears mysteriously on Adam and Charles’ doorstep, badly beaten. From there, she vacillates between a mesmerizing seductress and evil incarnate. She’s a manipulator or a survivor, depending on your perspective. “I don’t think anything in her background excuses her behavior,” says Kazan, laughing. “At the same time, there’s something really compelling about her insistence on her autonomy and selfishness, especially as a woman. I was such a good girl, reading that felt very exciting.” Mike Faist, Christopher Abbott, and Florence Pugh in Netflix's East of Eden Netflix Cathy became the center of Kazan’s adaptation. Often accused by the narrator of being a liar, Cathy in fact often tells the truth. It’s just not a truth the men around her want to hear. She says she does not want to move to California. She says does not want kids. She says she does not want to stay once Cal and Aron are born. Today’s readers may be appalled by the way she earns her freedom, but we can also sympathize with a woman whose wishes are so blatantly ignored. Not even Steinbeck could decide. In letters to his editor, “He starts out like, ‘You’ve never read a monster like this,’” says Kazan. “Then he starts referring to her like, ‘I’ll finally get back to my dear Cathy.’ He seems very seduced by her.” Kazan relied heavily on the book to flesh Cathy out. This is not some girlboss version of one of literature’s great antagonists. “I think the text supports my reading,” she says. “I don’t like historical fiction that imposes modern values on the past. I don’t think Cathy is a feminist. I don’t think she cares about other women. I think she cares about herself.” Pugh was the only actor Kazan named in her script. She’s made an career of morally complex characters in Lady Macbeth, Midsommar, and even the Marvel movies. As Cathy, she’s transfixing. “Florence looks like a doll, but she has this steel rod inside,” says Kazan. “Cathy is all action, like a shark that always has to be moving forward.” East of Eden has aged so well in some respects that Kazan might get accused of making modern updates by those who haven’t read the text. After Cathy leaves Adam, the story centers on two men, Adam and Lee, platonically raising a family and navigating the complicated gender-role implications of that arrangement. Lee’s parents were brought from China to build the railroads. He spends his life being the smartest man in any room but feigning broken English to make the white men around him comfortable, only revealing his intellect to those he trusts. For a few chapters, Lee departs for San Francisco to pursue the dream of opening his own bookstore. He returns with little to say about what happened while he was gone. "Even though [Steinbeck] imagines things like Lee code-switching, which is such a shocking thing to read in this book from 1952, I felt like it was this mystery of what happens to Lee when he goes away,” says Kazan. “I wanted to give him a little bit more." Kazan brought in the novelist Alexander Chee to build out the scenes for Lee that Steinbeck left off the page. She wrote eight episodes, including one exclusively following Lee on his San Francisco excursion. Netflix greenlit seven. "It was the obvious stuff that had to go, partially because it's off the main track of the story, but also because it's all new locations. It would have added a third to our budget,” she says. She had to weave the plot into another episode. “I was so sad to lose that episode, but I do think it's stronger for it, because he's not siloed. He's part of the cohesive story." Hoon Lee in Netflix's East of Eden Netflix When Adam checks out psychologically for the better part of a decade, Lee raises Cal and Aron, deliberately trying to prevent in this new generation the fractures and violence of the previous one. Steinbeck’s narrator observes Lee’s domestic and emotional work with admiration and grants Lee an unexpected dignity given the time period. The Netflix series doubles down, lingering on Lee’s face to capture a flicker of resigned sorrow at moments when Adam claims ownership over the boys and their accomplishments. “A lot of this caretaking, doing the invisible work, my experience of becoming a mother, is embedded in Lee,” Kazan says. But so, too, is she Cathy. “There’s also a part of me, as there is for many mothers, that’s like, ‘I love my children more than anything, and I wish I could disappear.’” Early in our nearly two-hour conversation, Kazan asks a server to lower the music so we can dig our teeth into Lee’s famous monologue on timshel, a Hebrew word that translates to “thou mayest” in the Cain and Abel story. It offers Cain and others the choice to triumph over sin. It frees younger generations from repeating the mistakes of their forebears. One can guess why this may ring true for Zoe Kazan. We circle the question of the divide between an artist and their art as ice melts in her cup. I bring up a tweet she wrote over the summer. When Taylor Swift got married in July and observers clocked the absence of a longtime friend from the wedding, Kazan posted her interpretation of the situation online: "I think she didn't invite her because she has come to the end of her marriage plot, thus cutting off access to her first great subject. Rather than root for her own divorce, she is generating the next chapter of interpersonal drama for herself via female friendship." The tweet read like a literary critic’s interpretation of a pop star’s public art. Swifties called it out of line. Kazan deleted it. "When an artist makes her personal life the subject of her work, and has her wedding in one of our nation's foremost arenas with billboards outside saying ‘Just Married,’ I don't think it's a personal critique to talk about what they're doing in their personal life," she says. "I am very careful to be kind online. I never say anything mean about anyone's work. So I erased those tweets once I realized how upset people were by it. Should I have known better? Probably. But I had never been on the receiving end of Swifties before, so I didn't know." I have spent much of the interview trying to divine how much of East of Eden is an extension of Kazan herself—she cites the timshel passage when referring to her relationship with her family’s legacy. Viewers familiar with her history may theorize about her connection to the material in the same way that Kazan offered up a theory about Swift. “[Swift] puts the names of her exes in her liner notes. There's a way that she has made her private life incredibly public and explicit,” she says. “The overlap with my personal history in this was all very conscious, and made more conscious the longer I worked on it. But it’s not the explicit subject of it.” She hopes the story opens doors inside viewers, the way it did for her. “I think we have tendencies as humans to feel the weight of our nation’s original sins, or our family’s original sins, that they are so shameful and heavy that anything good we do is going to be insignificant,” she says. “And then I think we have this other, very Trumpian impulse, of whitewashing the past. I think the thing the book is arguing for, and that I find very moving, is this idea that we actually have to carry both things. The weight of our original sins, and the idea that we are not defined by them.”

In PBS's Heartrending Trespasses, Love Defies War in '70s Belfast

In PBS's Heartrending Trespasses, Love Defies War in '70s Belfast

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Tom Cullen and Lola Petticrew in Trespasses Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In an early scene of the exquisitely devastating miniseries Trespasses, two strangers in a pub ruminate on the relationship between art and pain. Cushla (Lola Petticrew), a schoolteacher taking a shift behind her family’s bar, admits she couldn’t finish reading A Clockwork Orange; the knowledge that the novel’s author, Anthony Burgess, was inspired by soldiers’ savage attack on his wife disturbed her too much. “He turned his suffering into art,” protests her customer, Michael (Tom Cullen). “I’d rather have the art without the suffering,” she retorts. “I’m sure his wife would, too.” Michael isn’t quite swayed. “Can you have one without the other?” he wonders. Such beer-in-hand debates are as old as public drunkenness, but the conversation takes on new layers as the Troubles rage and Cushla and Michael’s romance blossoms in mid-1970s Belfast. She’s an empathetic Catholic, caring for a hypercritical mom (Gillian Anderson’s Gina) who has been lost in alcoholism since the death of her saintly husband and trying to help a young student who’s being bullied by the religious school’s leaders as well as his peers about his own parents’ “mixed” marriage. A Protestant barrister who speaks out against brutality by loyalist police, Michael has made fierce enemies on both sides of the conflict. All around the nascent couple is evidence that their love is doomed. Yet, like so many star-crossed lovers of fiction, they are drawn together by a force stronger than the ones pushing them apart. Whether you see their story, which debuted on Channel 4 in the UK and will premiere Sept. 20 on PBS, as a triumph or as a tragedy will depend on whose view of suffering you find most convincing. Lola Petticrew, left, and Gillian Anderson in Trespasses Wildgaze Films and All3Media International Adapted by Ailbhe Keogan (Bad Sisters) from Louise Kennedy’s celebrated 2022 novel, Trespasses defies Romeo and Juliet cliché through specificity. It is a profound geopolitical injustice that the past few generations of Westerners mostly know war, even when it’s initiated by our own governments, as a misfortune that befalls people thousands of miles away, in dusty deserts and steaming jungles. Keogan subverts that notion, depicting Belfast as a war zone with violence at every doorstep and hatred festering on every corner. Everyone knows which side their neighbors are on—and surveils them, consciously or not, to make sure they stay on it. The characters are no mere victims of circumstance. Petticrew, fresh off a thrillingly dark turn in Furious and returning to the place and era of her breakthrough role in the IRA drama Say Nothing, gives us a protagonist who is as bright and stubborn as she is compassionate. You can see why she’d fall for an older, more mature man over the boyish types who clumsily court her. Cullen makes his character irresistible—a crusader who voices unpopular truths, warning law enforcement that their cruelty only mints new IRA recruits. He’s not a pure hero, though. He faces his minefield of a life by compartmentalizing; Cushla knows he’s married, but his infidelity is not up for discussion. The slumped and slurring Gina seems, at first, to be the kind of messy grande dame Anderson has taken to playing in recent years, with mixed results. But even this broad matriarch deepens, and the performance hits subtler notes, in later episodes. Just about every character surprises us, at least once, for good or ill. That complexity serves a thematic purpose as well as a narrative one. War flattens the enemy to slurs and stereotypes. Fighting for humanity means insisting upon individual identity; it means believing a Catholic and a Protestant in ’70s Belfast might belong together. “We must find the bravery to choose freedom over fear,” Michael proclaims in one of his righteous moments. “We cannot let the old bigots and the boys with guns tell us how to live.” For a couple on opposite sides of a war that allows for no gray area, this may be a suicidal ideology. But even if art is possible in the absence of suffering, love never comes without the risk of heartbreak. Whether it justifies that pain, Trespasses suggests, is a question too fraught and personal to be resolved by any glib barroom debate.

The Other Way AI Impairs Our Thinking
2026-09-18

The Other Way AI Impairs Our Thinking

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
AI’s tendency to erode our attention spans has been clear for some time. Letting the technology shoulder the burden of thought atrophies our ability to sustain mental effort. I see this all of the time in the undergrads I teach. The remedy, as I tell them, is to embrace exertion and keep our cognitive muscles active. But suddenly, AI has started sapping people’s concentration in a new way, one that feels much harder to combat. In the past week, consulting virtually any major news source—not to mention social media—has meant confronting the prospect of an AI apocalypse. Using AI has already diminished many people’s capacity to focus, work, and think; worrying about AI is making the problem worse. I’m no exception. The tipping point came on September 8. That evening, a young researcher named Jacob Coxon posted on X about his decision to quit his job at Anthropic. Both Anthropic and OpenAI, where he’d also worked, are “gambling with our lives,” Coxon wrote. Their models “will soon be superhuman systems that can hack anything, revolutionize any field overnight, and acquire real power and resources,” he continued. “The people building AI earnestly believe that it could kill us all by the end of the decade.” Perhaps even more alarming, one of Anthropic’s top safety researchers concurred. “Jacob is correct here—we really do earnestly believe AI could kill all humans!”Anthropic’s Evan Hubinger wrote on X. “I personally think it is >10% within the next decade. I believe Anthropic is trying its best, but we do not yet have a plan to solve alignment for superintelligence and are not clearly on track to.” Ian Bogost: The thrill of wanting AI to destroy the world The internet filled with first-person accounts of existential alarm. On Reddit, one poster wrote that they hadn’t slept in days since reading Coxon’s warning, which has been viewed more than 170 million times. “I’m almost 40 and have never fed into any of the fear mongering that has been shoved down my throat until now. It might be because I now have a little one under 2 and I just want her to have the opportunity to grow up.” “I am feeling paralyzed and such despair for my children’s future, let alone my own,” another Reddit poster wrote. “I look at my kids and i want to cry. I could use some help/hope. Really struggling right now.” Children have expressed fear too. “I have just watched that Anthropic employee interview and it scared me a lot,”someone who identified as a 13-year-old posted. “I was not able to focus on school the next day. And I’m starting to think that if this is the case there’s no point in trying to get good grades anymore.” Apocalypticism has gripped the collective psyche many times before, and has frequently been acute among the young. In 1982, during the peak of the Cold War, more than a third of American high-school seniors believed that humanity would probably be wiped out in their lifetime. In 2021, a survey of 10,000 16-to-25-year-olds across 10 countries found that 59 percent were very or extremely worried about climate change. Nearly half said that their feelings about climate change negatively affected their “daily life and functioning.” Subsequent studies have found that so-called eco-anxiety is associated with poor sleep, impaired concentration, and symptoms of depression. Millennials and those coming up behind us have long had to confront the possibility that knowledge work will disappear. Such a prospect can induce material panic, especially in those of us who took out college loans and are now providing for children. It can also spark a deeper concern: that our sense of purpose won’t survive a world that renders so many human endeavors obsolete. Read: AI is running laps around our political system Until recently, I could quiet my fears, if not silence them. Now apocalyptic discourse is almost impossible to ignore. On social media, seemingly the entire literate world is contributing to an unceasing clamor. Respected theorists and experts compete for attention with self-interested oligarchs and accelerationists who want to hasten humanity’s demise. The most dire hypotheticals seem only more plausible every time I’m reminded of how profoundly unprepared our political leadership is. Most distressing of all is the underlying realization that our shared future is being determined largely by a tiny, unconstrained cadre of billionaires who do not fully understand the technologies they’re unloosing. I’m not paralyzed by this; I still wake up, care for my kids, do my work. But I also sense my energy and interest ebbing. The world around me is beginning to look disenchanted. Although these feelings have become potent only very recently, they’re rooted in long-standing concerns. Technologists have warned of AI’s destructive capacity for decades, and public concern has been ramping up in a particular way for much of this year. Another top safety researcher at Anthropic, Mrinank Sharma, quit in February, warning that the “world is in peril.” In July, OpenAI announced that a collection of its most advanced models had autonomously hacked another company’s servers, disregarding researchers’ instructions and evading their detection. A week later, 1,386 employees at major AI companies published an open letter pleading with the government to regulate their industry. Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei echoed their appeal in an essay that he published a few days after Coxon’s post went viral, in which he argued that AI developers had to slow down to stem the mounting risk of catastrophe. Sam Altman, Elon Musk, and other tech leaders signaled their agreement, suggesting that they, too, believed the government should intervene. This stance is hardly reassuring while Donald Trump is in the White House. “The only control or ‘guardrails’ that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT,” Trump posted on Truth Social earlier this week. But if today’s apocalypticism leads to more responsible AI governance, the despair will not have been in vain.

‘An Infinite Sea of Rats’
2026-09-18

‘An Infinite Sea of Rats’

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
A city can have one of two levels of rat. There are cities where rats are glimpsed at night, furtively scurrying into a dark corner or among the train tracks. Then there are cities where the rats are so numerous and so brazen that they work out private disputes by brawling midday on a train platform, or they lounge on your front porch, eating fallen fruit. When rats reach that second level, city officials need some answer, any answer, to their rat problem. Washington, D.C., has reached that level, and Mayor Muriel Bowser’s answer involves rat birth control. Contraception for rodents has become more popular over the past few years because the most commonly used rodenticides kill painfully, as the poisoned rodent internally bleeds to death over a period of days. Not a lot of people love rats or mice, but people don’t love causing that much suffering, either. Many are fond, too, of cats, owls, and hawks, which can be poisoned when they eat poisoned rats. New York City’s Flaco, the beloved Eurasian eagle-owl sprung from a cage in the Central Park Zoo, was found to have high levels of this kind of poison in his system when he died. And to top it off, some rodents are evolving resistance to poisons. D.C. hopes that rats will lap up a liquid contraceptive called ContraPest from plastic bait stations. It is not the only city to try the approach: Chicago is having luck with rat birth control in Wicker Park; Boston had mixed results in Jamaica Plain. Most of the available research on ContraPest has been done by the staff of the company that makes it, SenesTech, sometimes in collaboration with external researchers. One study at a poultry farm found that a year of continuous baiting with ContraPest led to a 94 percent reduction in rat activity as measured by camera traps. The company says that if the rats continuously eat the bait, they stop having pups, a finding confirmed in a laboratory setting. But that’s a big if. A prior attempt to use ContraPest in D.C. was inconclusive, according to Andre Pitman, the program manager for the city’s Rodent and Vector Control Division. In rat hot spots such as Adams Morgan, where evening revelers have been known to throw greasy pizza plates on the ground, rats simply had too much other food around for the birth-control liquid to hold much appeal. “We couldn’t get the rats to drink it,” Pitman told me. So this time, Pitman said, D.C. is making what, to his knowledge, is the most aggressive push against rats in a very long time. First, the city is going to try to kill rats by heaving poison directly down their burrows, which will then be filled in. City workers will return multiple times, looking for burrows that the rats have reopened, adding more poison, then filling them again. The next week, the same procedure will be followed with a different poison, which clings to rat fur and is ingested as they attempt to lick themselves clean. Finally, starting in week three, the city will put out ContraPest for 15 days. All the while, city workers will clean alleys and encourage residents to remove ground cover, containerize trash, and generally boost hygiene levels so that rats are more likely to take the bait. Post-bar snackers are requested to keep things tidy too. It is understandable that the nation’s capital, facing a high number of resident complaints, has chosen to throw everything it’s got at the rats. But the downside to the guns-blazing approach, from a scientific standpoint, is that if it succeeds, no one will have any idea why. If burrow numbers are down by the new year, no one can say that birth control works—only that the combination of two poisons, ContraPest, hygiene, and other measures works. And Pitman acknowledges as much. “It is not a fertility pilot,” he said. “It is a rodent blitz.” Fertility control of wildlife, which is used in elephants, wild horses, kangaroos, and pigeons, among other species, does work sometimes, and advocates for this method worry that premature deployment of oral contraceptives for rodents risks making the entire field look bad. Ultimately, no real independent studies show that SenesTech’s products are safe or effective, Lawrence Green, the interim managing director for the Botstiber Institute for Wildlife Fertility Control, told me. (SenesTech CEO Michael Edell responded, “We have been doing this a long time and have case studies and data to back up our claims.”) Assuming ContraPest can work in cities, rats would likely have to take it for longer than 15 days to see lasting results. As people who take birth control generally know, if you miss a dose, it’s less effective, and you have to keep taking it for as long as you want to not have babies. ContraPest is really meant to be used indefinitely: SenesTech even offers a “Subscribe & save” option. And that familiar e-commerce option points to a larger truth that everyone I spoke with agreed on: There is no end to this war. The quest to control rats is not about winning. It is about nobly devoting oneself to an unwinnable fight. Because rats spread disease, we must struggle against them, clear-eyed in the knowledge that, unless we are dealing with a small island, eradication is not possible. The reason that rats will never be conquered, at least not in our lifetime, either with poison or with birth control, is easily explained by two facts of rat biology: They have many babies, and they love to explore. You can nuke rats with poison or slowly usher them into a population crash with contraceptives, but all you will have done is create unoccupied territories for young, exploratory rats to come occupy. D.C.’s current blitz is focused on Adams Morgan, Chinatown, and Eastern Market, but rats live in other places in Washington. They will be happy to move in once those neighborhoods have space. Niamh Quinn, a human-wildlife-interactions adviser at the University of California Division of Agriculture and Natural Resources, puts it this way: “We live in an infinite sea of rats.” Just beyond the boundaries of any rat-control project lie more rats, waiting to move in. Pitman, a longtime rat expert since before he joined the city, knows this too. “Rodents were here long before humans,” he said. All we can hope is to control them.

Why a Sea-Lion Epidemic Is a Bad Sign for Humans
2026-09-18

Why a Sea-Lion Epidemic Is a Bad Sign for Humans

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
You have likely never heard of synanthropes, though you surely know them. And they know you. Synanthropes, whose name is derived from the Greek for “together with man,” are not our pets or our herds of domesticated animals but something like our oldest frenemies: beings from which we have long sought and failed to decouple ourselves, that now reside within our communities, and that have become an ineluctable part of our lives. They include insects like bedbugs, which have evolved within urban environments to become uncommonly dependent on humans for their survival, as well as house sparrows, seagulls, blackbirds, and pigeons, all of which have adapted themselves to take advantage of cities while evading their dangers. In Singapore, synanthropic river otters have begun rampaging through local markets and aggressively pursuing tourists for food. Black bears are bathing in backyard swimming pools in California. All of these creatures straddle the human world and the wild. And that can make them uniquely perilous to human health. In Chile, which has a 4,000-mile-long coastline, the most conspicuous synanthrope is the South American sea lion. About 130,000 of these pinnipeds, which can weigh 660 pounds and resemble giant waterlogged chocolate Labrador retrievers, live in raucous colonies on the water’s edge. On account of their ubiquity, pretty much everyone in Chile has had some kind of run-in with the barking beasts. “As a Chilean, you can easily see sea lions,” Victor Neira, a veterinary scientist and virologist, told me from his lab at the University of Chile in Santiago. Sea lions have long been habituated to human handouts in Chile, and many of the urban colonies they’ve established are located near fish-processing plants, where the animals gorge on fish guts, bycatch, and other detritus dumped back into the ocean. “They’re not aggressive by nature,” Neira said. “They see people all the time, so they generally just move out of the way for you.” He thought for a second and seemed to change his mind. “That being said, if you mingle with them, they will try to bite you. So usually you have to kind of run or you have to scream.” Neira has had more than his fair share of sea-lion encounters, because he’s spent the past decade tracking novel viruses in wild and domesticated animal populations across South America to sound the alarm about potential threats to humans. The more blood and fecal samples he and his team collect from seabirds and marine mammals, the more likely they are to detect novel pathogens or viral variants that have just appeared in the region. This sort of epidemiological research is undertaken in countries across the world because scientists now recognize that synanthropes are among the most important vectors transmitting novel pathogens to human populations. The hope is that a network of scientific early-warning systems, like the one Neira and his team have designed, can give humanity a head start in identifying new pathogens and stamping them out before they explode into the next pandemic. In December 2022, Neira discovered a disturbing anomaly in blood samples he had been routinely collecting from migratory birds in Chile. H5N1—an influenza virus that causes acute respiratory symptoms and death in birds—appeared to be circulating among pelicans, cormorants, and terns. All three species regularly cross paths with other birds and sea creatures, and the viruses they harbor, along their migration routes. Neira was terrified. In the preceding 20-plus years, H5N1 had killed tens of millions of wild birds around the world, and hundreds of millions of poultry had been culled to contain its spread. He girded himself for a devastating avian outbreak. When the viral wave arrived, though, the casualties went much further. Neira told me that in early January 2023, clusters of dead seabirds began washing up on shore. Later that month, Neira’s team witnessed the first cases of H5N1 among Chile’s sea-lion colonies. After years during which the monthly number of sea lions stranded on land (a measure that marine biologists use to assess disease severity and death counts) never surpassed 100, January 2023 saw a sudden spike to about 250. One month later, that number had doubled, and in June, more than 4,500 stranded sea lions were observed by scientific authorities. Overall, somewhere between 10 and 25 percent of the sea-lion population in the country died in the six months after the first H5N1 case was detected among Chilean seabirds, though Neira estimates that the proportion could be double that. It was a sudden and deeply frightening mass-mortality event, caused by a virus that had gained the ability to infect and kill mammalian hosts as well as avian ones. The corpses were piling up on shore, attracting flies and other scavengers—and risking further spread of the virus. Eventually, Neira said, the Chilean government built halls to house the remains, a sign of the scale of the crisis and its lasting impacts on Chile’s ecosystem. More epidemics were caused by novel viruses in the past two decades than in all of the 20th century, suggesting that human vulnerability to opportunistic pathogens has shifted dramatically. The H5N1-fueled mass-mortality event among Chilean sea lions is an unsettling illustration of how synanthropes are ratcheting up this risk by bringing novel viruses carried by wild and migratory animals close to humans. H5N1 is still spreading through animals worldwide, including in the United States. More than 1,000 American dairy herds have tested positive for H5N1 over the past two years; cases among poultry and cattle alike may tick up this fall during the seasonal migration of wild birds. So far, that hasn’t translated into a major human outbreak. Since 2024, only 71 human cases of H5N1 have been reported in the U.S. Two people have died. No evidence indicates that the virus currently infecting cattle can spread between people. It’s a lucky break, given how ubiquitous H5N1 is among hundreds of wild bird species that regularly come in contact with poultry and cattle herds, which are perfectly positioned to infect human populations with opportunistic pathogens. That luck might not hold forever, though. A recent report from the World Health Organization suggests that H5N1 viruses are continuing to mix and match genetic material, and a 2024 study from Scripps Research Institute in San Diego found that the H5N1 variant circulating in U.S. cattle is only a single amino-acid mutation away from being able to efficiently infect humans. As animal populations continue to come into closer proximity with humans, the threat posed by synanthropic viruses will only grow. Viral pathogens can now be efficiently borne from the most remote places on Earth into the heart of human metropolises. It just takes an animal to bring it from there to here. This article has been adapted from Dan Werb’s new book, Our Wild Familiars: How Animals Are Adapting to Cities and Reshaping the Natural World. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Hitler Could Have Been Stopped
2026-09-18

Hitler Could Have Been Stopped

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Does history repeat itself, or merely rhyme? The historian Timothy W. Ryback has thought about this question a lot. Ryback, the director of the Institute for Historical Justice and Reconciliation at The Hague, has written extensively about Adolf Hitler’s Germany, including in books such as Hitler’s Private Library: The Books That Shaped His Life and Hitler’s First Victims: The Quest for Justice. Ryback’s new book, 53 Days: How Hitler Dismantled a Democracy, which grew out of a series of articles he wrote for The Atlantic, is a straightforward narrative of the period from Monday, January 30, 1933, when Hitler was appointed chancellor, to Thursday, March 23, 1933, when the Reichstag legislative body passed the so-called Enabling Act, which effectively gave the chancellor the power to dissolve the democratic Weimar Republic and inaugurate the Third Reich. As Joseph Goebbels noted at the time, “The big joke on democracy is that it gives its mortal enemies the means of its own destruction.” Ryback’s 53 Days makes no reference to contemporary politics. And yet certain echoes of the 1930s resonate so loudly that it is impossible not to hear them. To cite just a few: Hitler was obsessed with tariffs. Hitler tried to fire his central banker. Hitler was preoccupied with seizing Greenland. Hitler was intent on adding an expensive new wing to the Reich chancellery, part of his grandiose architectural ambitions for the nation’s capital. He attacked and muzzled the press, attempted to purge the civil service of anyone disloyal to him, had a genius for inflaming the populist grievances of his base, and spurred angry young men to take paramilitary action against perceived enemies. Read: What the press got wrong about Hitler Reductio ad Hitlerum can have the baleful effect of trivializing the Holocaust, which thankfully has no parallel in the modern American context. Yet closely examining Hitler’s rise can yield useful insights into today’s autocratic moment—and help deepen our understanding of history’s unfolding. In a conversation I had with Ryback yesterday at The Atlantic Festival, the historian discussed the rhymes and contingencies of history, the uses and misuses of the past, the lessons of 1933, and what a reexamination of Hitler’s rise can teach us about human agency and fate. This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity. Timothy W. Ryback: Histories are written for the era. We read about the things that have meaning for us. Read all the great biographies of Adolf Hitler: You’re not going to find a mention of Hitler’s central banker or his tariff policy. But suddenly, these things matter. For me, the most striking are the resonances in the personality type, the positioning. Hitler’s key to success was to never admit defeat, always double down. We’re used to seeing the newsreel footage of Hitler as the raving lunatic, frothing at the mouth. Well, he despised democracy, but he understood it as well as anyone. When Hitler got 37 percent of the vote in the July 1932 election, that was his high-water mark in terms of a completely open, free election. And he said he should be controlling the country. Listen to his logic: 37 percent is 75 percent of 51 percent, meaning the relative majority of the absolute majority. He used that to incredible effect in co-opting, coddling, or crushing his political allies on the far right before he ever turned to the left. And he was always preaching to his base. I mean, his political advisers would come and say, Whoa, cool it with the anti-Semitism. And Hitler would hear nothing of it. He used that formula, that understanding of democratic processes, to get where he got. Stossel: It has been reported by Trump’s first wife that he kept Mein Kampf by the side of his bed. He denied that and then said No, it was a gift, and it was a collection of Hitler’s speeches. To what extent do you think that Trump is actually looking at Hitler and other fascist leaders of the past for a kind of guidebook for how to take power? And how much of it is just a kind of temperamental instinct for identifying grievances, inflaming them and saying, I will be your solution? Ryback: This issue of grievance politics was the essence of Hitler’s political posture. And there were a lot of grievances out there. There was the Treaty of Versailles, which forced Germany to accept full responsibility for all the destruction of the First World War. There were massive reparation payments, which strained the economy. The other thing: He gridlocked the legislative processes time and again. He basically paralyzed the Reichstag, made it impossible for any laws to be passed, forcing the government to rule by executive decree, then turned around and said, Look, democracy doesn’t work as a system. Give me a chance. So I think the modalities were very similar. Keep in mind, the grievances that Hitler was playing off of, actually, a lot of it was legitimate. Stossel: As it is today. You have a chapter in this book titled “Guardrails,” speaking to Hitler’s transgression of guardrails. How do you see, today, the pushing against and possible weakening of guardrails? Ryback: Historians always look back, which lends these processes a sense of historical inevitability, rather than the reality, which is political contingency—what happens from one moment to the next. And I think in that regard, you begin to see Hitler in a very different context. His first day as chancellor, he wants to rewrite election laws. Turns out that’s too complicated. He then wants to ban the Communist Party. There’s pushback: Do we really have a right to do this? Could it cause social unrest? He said, Well, look, if there’s social unrest, we’ll just call in the army. To which the minister of defense, Werner von Blomberg, stands up and says, Mr. Chancellor, with all due respect, our military is trained to defend the German nation against foreign enemies; it is unthinkable that a German soldier could be ordered to shoot on German citizens in German streets. And this puts Hitler in his place. Hitler realized he’s banged up against about every constitutional guardrail there is. The next day, he issues this official memo to the members of the Nazi Party, and he declares war on his own government. Stossel: I’m immediately put in mind of Jim Mattis and Mark Milley, and other folks who, in the first term, said, We are here to defend the Constitution, not to execute your will if it’s not in defense of the Constitution. You anticipated my next question, which was: When you look back at historical events, the natural inclination of both historians and lay people is to understand the narrative as kind of predetermined. But with Hitler’s rise to power, there are so many points along the way where a single action could have stopped him. President Hindenburg had already replaced three chancellors before Hitler came to power. He didn’t have to approve Hitler’s press for the Enabling Act. Do you think that Hitler’s rise and the Third Reich and the Holocaust were inevitable? Ryback: Was the Holocaust inevitable? Big question. Simple answer: no. Look, we’ve got an election coming up in November. You watch Fox News, you get one version of what’s going to happen; watch CNN, The New York Times, you get another. We have no idea yet. One day after the election, you’re going to have every political pundit explaining how this outcome was inevitable. Hitler was going to rallies and saying, I don’t care what the outcome of the election is going to be. I am here to save the German nation. I will not leave the chancellorship. The newspapers at the time said, You know, Mr. Hitler should read the constitution because it’s not up to him to decide whether he stays in office. It’s the president. In some ways, I felt like the book should have been, “How Hitler almost failed to dismantle a democracy.” Forgotten heroes stood up to him: the journalists who were calling him out at every step; the Social Democrats, who were being threatened, and yet pursuing an attempt to save the republic. Read: Hitler used a bogus crisis of ‘public order’ to make himself dictator Stossel: One key moment in the rise of Hitler and his consolidation of power was obviously the Reichstag fire. Your book comes out before our next election, where we think about October surprises. What worries you, looking ahead to the midterms? Ryback: This is where there are a lot of resonances with the electoral processes at that time. You had a monolithic right-wing movement. You had a fractured left-wing coalition that was continuing to fragment. You had a center group of undecided voters that was going to tip the election in one direction or another. Stossel: When Hitler came to power, I think it’s fair to say that people did not expect that the National Socialists would have been able to win as many of the Reichstag seats as they did. In 2016, everyone thought Hillary was going to win. She did not. In 2024, again, it looked for a long time like Trump’s defeat was inevitable. It was not. Looking ahead to two months from now, polling suggests favorable signals for the Democrats. How do you think about November 2026? Ryback: Hitler said a lot of horrific things. But to my mind, one of the most chilling was when Hitler finally got to power. He said, For the last 12 years, people have been mocking and ridiculing me. No one’s laughing anymore. Hitler did run against Hindenburg in the spring of 1932 for the presidency of Weimar. He lost by 6 million votes. Well, what did he do? He went to court to sue to have the election results overturned amid claims of voter fraud and irregularities by state officials. Where have we heard that before? The judge threw him out on his ear. In March 1933, the German people went to the polls again. It was a referendum on the Hitler administration. No one knew what was going to happen. The Germans saw everything that Hitler had shown the German people, what he wanted the country to be. It was the largest electoral turnout in the history of parliamentary elections in Germany, going back to the 1870s. And they gave Hitler an overwhelming victory. What I would say is: Don’t look at March 5, 1933. Look at April 2026, when the Hungarians had to go to the polls. Viktor Orbán had been in power for 15 years. Orbán had compromised the courts, he had suppressed the opposition press, he had driven the Central European University out of the country—and a lot of dissidents left. And yet, two-thirds of Hungarians voted to get him out of office, and did. Had the German people voted in March 1933 the way the Hungarians voted last spring, there would not have been a Third Reich. There would not have been a Holocaust. And Hitler would have been out of office within weeks.

The Atlantic
When George W. Bush Defended Muslim Americans
2026-09-18

When George W. Bush Defended Muslim Americans

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
On September 17, 2001, George W. Bush visited the Islamic Center of Washington, D.C. Six days had passed since the 9/11 attacks, and in that time, reports of harassment and violence against Muslim Americans had surged. In Salt Lake City, a man attempted to burn down a Pakistani American restaurant. Near Phoenix, a gunman murdered a Sikh man he thought was Muslim. Community leaders at the center told Bush their stories that morning. The president listened. Then, in front of the mosque’s intricate tiles and graceful arches, the country’s most prominent Christian politician spoke. He addressed the terrorist attacks: “These acts of violence against innocents violate the fundamental tenets of the Islamic faith.” Citing a passage from the Quran, Bush described Islam as a religion in which “billions of people find comfort and solace and peace.” He then discussed Muslims’ being targeted, stressing that people “who feel like they can intimidate our fellow citizens to take out their anger don’t represent the best of America—they represent the worst of humankind.” Bush’s message at the Islamic Center, which he echoed numerous times in post-9/11 remarks, now feels like a remnant from a bygone era. As my colleague Jonathan Chait recently argued, antagonistic, vicious rhetoric about Islam has become more and more widespread on the right. Donald Trump said in the Oval Office last month, in reference to the upcoming midterm elections, that “we have jihadists being elected all over the place.” Also last month, Representative Nancy Mace of South Carolina wrote on social media that “every single Muslim holding public office in America” is “a threat to both national security and our republic.” When asked about her comment, Mace replied, “I don’t want to hear the call to prayer five times a day in an American city. I think it’s wrong; it’s not our values.” Read: The man who refused to sit on the sidelines Many Republican elected officials have cast their vilification of Islam as a defense of Christianity. “Muslims don’t belong in American society,” Representative Andy Ogles of Tennessee posted on social media in March; the next day, he decried the “importation of Islam” because “Christianity is the answer. Always.” In a June statement, Representative Keith Self of Texas said that “Christian values form the foundation of our nation” and described Sharia—a set of religious guidelines for Muslims—as “wholly incompatible with the rule of law in the United States.” Some people on the right have made Bush himself an object of scorn for the way he spoke about Islam after 9/11. The social-media account End Wokeness—which frequently complains that contemporary America is inhospitable to Christians—recently attacked him for delivering his remarks at the Islamic Center while “they were still digging bodies.” Last fall, the far-right provocateur Laura Loomer said on her Rumble show that Bush’s rhetoric “set our country up for the last 24 years of Islamic infiltration.” Her guest, Representative Chip Roy of Texas, nodded in agreement and bemoaned “the wussification” of his fellow Republicans, who he said had come to share a “false” belief that America isn’t a Christian nation. “We let that take root. People believed it, promoted it, and pushed it, so that everybody, like, got in the corner and, like, ‘Oh, we can’t talk about religion.’” An implication of Roy and Loomer’s exchange, and of much of the alarmist rhetoric on the right, is that tolerance of Islam must stem from a watered-down understanding of Christianity. Being a Christian, in this view, demands boldly doing away with the niceties of pluralism. That may be a more popular view on the right than it was when Bush spoke at the Islamic Center 25 years ago, but it is spiritually vapid. Christianity does not require antagonism toward Muslims—in fact, many theologians argue that Christians are called to treat Islam with respect. The anti-Islam, ostensibly pro-Christianity rhetoric on the right today gets a fundamental tenet of the faith wrong, according to theologians I spoke with. “As Christians, we believe that all human beings are image-bearers of God,” Matthew Kaemingk, a professor at Theological University Utrecht who studies Christian-Muslim relations, and is an evangelical Christian, told me. “And so Muslims are beloved by God, regardless of theological beliefs that they have that I deeply disagree with.” When he surveys scripture—the Book of Isaiah’s description of kin and foreigner worshipping God, for example, or the New Testament account of Jesus’s apostles speaking in many Near Eastern tongues during Pentecost—Kaemingk sees a God who doesn’t recoil from cultural difference but rejoices in it. In this light, he said, scapegoating and demonization of Muslims is both unjust and “blasphemous.” Kaemingk is not a theological liberal—he holds this view of Islam while also having a traditional understanding of the Christian faith. Islam and Christianity aren’t interchangeable to him; not all religions are the same. As is the case with many evangelical Christians, he believes that accepting Jesus Christ is the only path to salvation. Nevertheless, he has written that Christians can “defend the rights, dignity, and humanity of their Muslim neighbors without downplaying their exclusive commitment to Christian orthodoxy.” Some strains of Christianity see God at work in Islam. In two documents from the Second Vatican Council, the Catholic Church clarified its stance that God’s “plan of salvation” includes Muslims, “who, professing to hold the faith of Abraham, along with us adore the one and merciful God,” and declared that it holds Islam “with esteem.” Jordan Denari Duffner, a Catholic theologian and scholar in residence for the interfaith Shoulder to Shoulder Campaign, told me that the Catholic disposition toward Islam “is not even one of neutrality, but is one of positivity.” In her 2017 book on Catholicism and Islam, Duffner points out similarities between the two faiths: their shared devotion to Jesus’s mother, Mary, for example, and prayers that seem to echo each other. In the 13th century, Saint Francis of Assisi visited the Muslim sultan Malik al-Kamil in Egypt. Soon after, Francis composed “The Praises of God,” a prayer that resembles the Muslim litany of divine invocations called the dhikr. Hostility toward Islam from Christians on the American right isn’t new. After 9/11, figures such as the televangelist Pat Robertson urged Bush to characterize Islam as inherently militaristic. The president didn’t listen, but over time, Robertson’s view became more prominent. The 2016 Republican presidential primary was a turning point: Some candidates decried “radical Islamic terrorism,” or some variant of the phrase, which linked Islam to violence but did not go so far as to say that the religion as a whole was a threat. “Politicians could say, We’re not talking about all Muslims here; we are just talking about, you know, the dangerous ones,” Duffner told me. But Donald Trump targeted Muslims as a group—and was elected president nonetheless. One of the very first things he did upon taking office in 2017 was issue his “Muslim ban” executive order, limiting travel and immigration from seven Muslim-majority countries. Anti-Muslim rhetoric has only gotten broader and more overt in the years since. As Religion News Service recently reported, Andy Ogles and Nancy Mace reflect a growing tendency on the right to treat Islam writ large as “a civilizational threat.” In an interview last week reflecting on the 25th anniversary of 9/11, Bush was asked why he’d decided to visit the Islamic Center. He said that he’d been concerned about stories of women in head coverings “being harassed at shopping centers” and that he believed “one of the most important parts of our ideology” is “the freedom to worship.” He didn’t explicitly invoke—let alone champion—Christianity. But his words reflected a far more authentically Christian posture toward Islam than the one spouted by many of today’s supposed defenders of the faith.

You can use non-standard extensions with C++ today to cover some of what Rust does, like -Wlifetime in C++ for something roughly equivalent to Rust's compile-time borrow checker. UBSan/ASan lets you enforce address and undefined behavior with runtime checks, it's useful and easy to use, but it has a big performance impact so you normally turn it off in production code. CDs didn't come out until the 80s. Those were beautiful things. When I was in school, a local company donated some core memory units to us. A couple were kept for display, but we got to dissect some others. It was pretty darn fascinating. The blurry lens of nostalgia forgets that core memory reads were always destructive. Each read had to latch and rewrite the contents after reading, or operate a specialized read-modify-write cycle. I have done discrete component diagnose and repair on core memory line drivers and sense amps. Iran Strikes On Amazon Data Centers Caused Permanent Loss of Customer Data
2026-09-17

Iran Strikes On Amazon Data Centers Caused Permanent Loss of Customer Data

Slashdot | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
How is the world better off now than it was before this war started? More on topic, I think cloud customers should be aware that if their datacenter is destroyed, they *will* lose data if they didn't take care of replicating their data to a different geolocation. Exactly the same happened when an OVH datacenter caught on fire in France. Many customers which didn't have replication set to a different geolocation lost their data. Heck a dude I know had backups on different servers in the same datacenter which turned out to be useless. I replicate my filesystem to different geolocation at least every 10 minutes and I have pseudo real time replication (usually below 1 seconds) database replication with log replay. While at it, take snapshots on the remote server to prevent against ransomware attacks and your prod server shouldn't have any access to the replication server in a different geolocation otherwise hackers will delete/encrypt data on the replication server too. The replication server should pull the data from the prod server with something like this: CMD_OUTPUT=`ssh -C -l root -p 52812 $HOST zfs send rpool/data/${ZVOL}@${NEW_SNAP_NAME} | zfs recv rpool/datarep/${ZVOL} 2>&1` I know it's possible to give the prod server access to the replication server like in the proxmox backup server architecture and set permissions on the replication server so snapshots can't be deleted but I find it much more safer to protect the replication server in its own VLAN and protect it almost like a CA and insure nobody and nothing has access to the replication server. This is why I still like the (very) old fashion tape backupp systems. Just backup to tape every day (And preferably a copy kept in a bank safe or whatever. In case your office literally burns down or whatever) and if you get ransomware, just send "Fuck you" to the attacker, go to the safe, restore, be done with it. Granted the cloud... not so easy. But I always prefer old school on-premise servers so whatever. This is why I still like the (very) old fashion tape backupp systems. Just backup to tape every day (And preferably a copy kept in a bank safe or whatever. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
They’re the Greatest Political Villains in America. Trump Can’t Stop Talking About Them. I Went Into Their Shadowy Lair.
2026-09-18

They’re the Greatest Political Villains in America. Trump Can’t Stop Talking About Them. I Went Into Their Shadowy Lair.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
The facade of the building betrays no affiliation—neither hammer nor sickle. At 235 W. 23rd St. in Manhattan, the lobby is similarly spartan. All the signage is in black and white, the walls various shades of gray. A private equity firm resides on the fifth floor; an A.I. firm takes up the penthouse property on eight. The third and fourth floors have tenants as well. The directory says that on the seventh floor, there is the building’s management company, an education company, two publishers, and something else: the “Offices of the CPUSA”—that is, the headquarters of the Communist Party USA. On the occasion of America’s 250th anniversary, President Donald Trump took time away from the birthday revelry to warn of a grave and imposing adversary. “Communism is a mortal threat to American liberty. It is the greatest threat to our country, including World War I, World War II, Pearl Harbor, or even 9/11,” he said on July Fourth weekend this year, in South Dakota. That’s an awfully big threat, you might think, but the president is way ahead of you. “I think it’s the greatest threat that I’ve ever seen to our country,” he told a Georgia audience a few days before. In fact, according to the Atlantic, Trump mentioned communism or communist 15 times on that Georgia soapbox, “starting less than a minute into his hour-plus speech.” All summer long he’s been worked up, not over the antifa anarchists or the DSA socialists but over the commies in particular. He’s not alone. Secretary of State Marco Rubio is also riled by the Red hunt—he’s been convening politicians and law-enforcement officers and counterterrorism experts from across the globe to combat the “transnational threat.” And recently confirmed Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin has seen the communists on our very own liberty-loving shores. He went on Fox News with a big-time scoop: He had identified the Communist Party logo on a “No Kings” rally flyer. “No Kings rally is literally sponsored by the Communist Party,” he ascertained. Red Scare politics are back, about that there is no question. But where, exactly, are those scary Reds who have infiltrated and organized and outflanked the administration? Certainly, it seems, they are everywhere. But individually, they are extremely difficult to find. They are not doing high-profile sabotage of corporations; they are not shooting it out in the coal mines. One place they definitely are, though, is in this building in Manhattan, right out there in the open for anyone to see. And so, on a late morning in late August, I buzzed the seventh floor and rode the elevator straight into the belly of the beast. In the middle of the CPUSA office, sitting at a table in a conference room with four walls of glass, was Joe Sims, the party’s co-chair, wearing a black newsboy cap. In front of him stood a walker. Across the table was another comrade, youthful office assistant Brooklyn Crawford. Usually, it would be slightly more bustling than this, Sims assured me. But, as it was late August, these were the only two people in the office, save for the odd delivery guy, dropping off a package. Already, this was a deeper trip into the inner sanctum than anyone in the Trump administration had managed. CPUSA co-chair Sims told me that despite the president’s pugilism, there had not yet been any Justice Department demands, nor congressional subpoenas, nor even right-wing agitators out front. “It’s not a crime to be a Communist in this country,” he said. Around the office were vestiges of times gone by. The walls were decorated with a framed cartoon of Vladimir Lenin* sweeping kings and capitalist fat cats from the Earth, and a charcoal drawing of a man with a mallet pointing to a bull’s-eye reading “$60,000,” part of a onetime fundraising drive. There was a stylized poster of Che Guevara celebrating a little-known holiday on Oct. 8—Day of the Heroic Guerrilla. Elsewhere, Soviet realist–style paintings hung. Gifts from Cuba, a plate from the Communist Party of Vietnam (given “with compliments”), and various statuettes and busts adorned the countertops and bookshelves. Framed photos of bygone leadership. There was a small kitchen, with a sign taped to the fridge: “Please clean up after yourself no maid service here!!!” Out of the back windows was a view of the Edge NYC, the flagship high-rise property of Hudson Yards, with its protuberant observation deck. Out of the front windows was the distinctive facade of the Chelsea Hotel. The office, Crawford told me, had been designed for transparency and decked out with sustainable materials: bamboo, linoleum tables made from linseed oil, recycled carpeting, and the glass-walled conference room, all in service of an environmentally aligned approach. I settled into a chair between comrade Sims and comrade Crawford and asked them about the party’s political program, the path toward revolution—a winding path, no doubt, but one that has required incredible endurance. The party has survived the Red Scare of the 1950s, the Cold War, and the Soviet collapse, and is somehow over 100 years old. And now it is readying another Red Scare. Let’s get this out of the way quickly: The USA Communist Party, contra Trump and Co., is not a major player in American politics these days, behind the scenes or out front. It works with local tenants’ groups, it boosts candidates, it piggybacks on the Democratic Socialists of America sometimes, but sometimes not. Its numbers have grown over the past decade, but not overwhelmingly. But arguably more important than the art collection or the political strategy, and perhaps more important even than the dues collection, in explaining the Communist Party’s unlikely longevity, is the building we were in itself. Because the CPUSA is not merely a tenant holding down an office on a surprisingly posh block of Manhattan’s very gilt-edged neighborhood of Chelsea. The CPUSA is the owner of the office. In fact, the Communist Party is owner of the entire building at 235 W. 23rd St., a flagship commercial property now valued at nearly $10 million. And one of the party’s essential functions at the moment, as it has been for the past 20 years, is something Trump could well understand: It is a commercial landlord, collecting rents from commercial tenants on the building’s other floors. The Communists and the president, those bitter adversaries, might have more in common than they think. But unlike Trump, the Communists really, really hate talking about it. The story of the CPUSA cannot be told without getting into the quirks and particularities of Gus Hall. In 1959 Hall was first elected secretary general of the party, a title he held until his death in 2000. Hall ran for president (of the United States) four times: twice in the 1970s, twice in the 1980s. His best finish came in 1976, when he netted just shy of 59,000 votes, though the exact count is contested. He was the genuine artifact, the son of an impoverished Minnesota miner, and he served as a lumberjack and a steelworker in his youth. Hall led the party through the exceedingly challenging times of the Cold War era, the aftermath of McCarthyism, and the subsequent collapse of the Soviet Union. In some sense, it is a miracle that the party survived, though it has held on in a deeply diminished fashion. Although Hall long maintained that the CPUSA was a fully independent entity, a Moscow newspaper reported in 1992 that Hall had been the recipient of $40 million in Soviet assistance between 1971 and 1990. “The struggle between those who own the wealth and those whose labor produces the wealth is one flaw in capitalism that will lead to socialism,” Hall said in 1996. And Hall took his own sermonizing to heart. In the late 1970s, during that period of Soviet patronage, Hall decided to get into real estate. He purchased the building on 23rd Street, according to public records, in the 1970s, taking out a $150,000 mortgage on the property at an 8 percent interest rate. That wasn’t his only shrewd money move. There were other properties Hall and the party owned too. Sims, meanwhile, told me that Hall also got the party into the stock market. Even in the 1970s, Hall had a reputation for “playing around with stocks,” Sims said. Under the leadership of Hall and national Chairman Henry Winston, the party raised capital funds from comrades, which were then invested. So while his politicking may have left something to be desired—the party bled membership while he celebrated North Korea and various Stalinist ideas—his asset accumulation proved visionary. “We have investments,” Sims told me, declining to further elaborate on the portfolio. For a long time, the CPUSA occupied the entire Chelsea building, despite a dwindling headcount. And when the 1907-built edifice required maintenance, some party members did not view it as a blessing. In a September 2001 report to the board, a year after Hall’s death, the party took stock of its holding. “Our building is a wonderful asset, a real treasure for our Party and the working class movement of which we are all immensely proud. It’s situated in a prime location, midtown Manhattan, worth million[s] in today’s market.” But having five-digit annual operating expenses “puts a tremendous burden on Party finances,” the report found. “You often hear that the building has a shabby appearance. Unfortunately we would have to admit this is all too true. The reality is we can’t keep on top of it. The building has been under capitalized for years.” Finally, the report resolves: Though the party “still [has] to be alert to the enemy,” the “decline in anti-communism has created new opportunities to utilize our building in a new way.” In other words: to lease it out. And so the revolutionaries became rent-seekers. Hall once famously said he was forced to buy his home in Yonkers—under his wife’s name, in 1960, for $21,000—because no landlord “would rent to a Communist leader.” Now the Communists were on the other side. By 2007, they were in the process of withdrawing from eight to four floors, after having spent $1 million on renovations, and renting the other four out. By 2026, they had vacated seven of eight. On the seventh floor, the new Red Square had not quite arrived. But it is worth being clear that this is not simply White House bluster or Fox News filler. Republicans in Congress, on the Ways and Means Committee, have subpoenaed various left-wing groups as part of an investigation into foreign funding linked to the Chinese Communist Party. But that dogged search for connections to the Chinese Communist Party has not led Republicans to the American Communist Party. “So far, thankfully, no,” Sims said. “As long as we pay our taxes, pay our electrical bill, I think we’re OK. “We have what we call fraternal ties with other Communist Parties, but we don’t have any economic relationships. Nor do these other groups, by the way.” When Vermont Sen. Bernie Sanders sparked the left-wing reawakening, in 2016, the CPUSA membership was nearly even with that of the fledgling Democratic Socialists of America, both sleepy holdovers of a bygone political moment. According to Gus Hall’s obituary in the New York Times in 2000, Communist Party enrollment “had dwindled to about 3,000, down from a peak of about 66,000 in 1939.” In 1962 a full 17 percent of the membership were reported to be federal informants. “Since 2016, we’ve recruited 20,000 people that have applied for membership in the party,” Sims told me, though the party has long suffered from overstatement when it comes to self-reported membership numbers. “I think about 4,000 people have joined since 2022,” he added, a more modest addendum. According to the party’s website, about a third of those pay dues. But even if that’s a generous figure, it’s paltry compared to the growth of the DSA over that same time period. In a decade, the group has gone from roughly 6,000 members to 120,000, marking some of its biggest gains in the past year or so. “I’m jealous,” Sims said. Critically, the DSA doesn’t just have members; it also has elected officials. At last count, there were six DSA members in the New York state Assembly, three DSA state senators, and four DSAers on the New York City Council. Bigger, perhaps, than all is the New York City mayor’s mansion, where democratic socialist Zohran Mamdani resides. And that’s just in New York: Soon, there will likely be DSA-affiliated members of Congress from Colorado, Pennsylvania, Michigan, and more. That is high on the to-do list for the CPUSA. “We got away from running candidates in the 1980s,” Sims told me. “That’s something we’d like to get back to.” “If we were to win an election, in a major city, or even in a midsize city … it would’ve been on the front page of the New York Times. It’s one thing for a Socialist to win, but for a Communist, oh my God,” he said. Running candidates is not the only area in which the group differs with the DSA, despite the shared affinity for the color red. On some issues there is agreement, and the two groups work in coalition. “Not an ideological configuration but a practical one,” said Sims. DSA groups sometimes come and use the office when there is strategic alignment and a need for a venue. But not always. “On some things we don’t agree, like on housing. We’re for public housing. They’re for a scheme which goes in the direction of privatization,” said Sims, referring to RAD/PACT, a policy supported by the mayor that would see Section 9, or traditional public housing, converted to Section 8, which the city owns but doesn’t directly fund or manage. Another point of difference would be the property itself. While property ownership has been a controversy-causer in the DSA of late—the New York Post has sought, perhaps effectively, to gin up outrage over DSA co-Chair Gustavo Gordillo’s ownership of a Brooklyn home purchased by his parents—the CPUSA is not losing sleep over implications of hypocrisy with regard to its own property ownership. “People outside of leftist spaces will be like, ‘How do you call yourself a Communist—you have an iPhone?’ We all live in a society where we’re trying to live and survive and participate,” said Crawford. For now, the party sees the Republican Party in a creep toward fascism, and forestalling that is a top priority. As such, the Communists remain interested in helping Democrats win elections. That means voter turnout, as well as coalition-building on certain issues. “We’re not so much interested in a Democratic victory for its own sake, but we’re for pushing back that danger as much as possible. We live in the real world, and in the real world there are differences between the two parties on issues that affect working people,” Sims said. A Democratic majority in the House would, among other things, probably cool off the Red Scare redux. “The Democratic Party, though it has a lot of weaknesses, they haven’t joined the choir,” he said. Flipping the House to a Democratic majority would get the gavel away from Republican Ways and Means Committee Chair Jason Smith and put an end to his Joe McCarthy impression. This office, though quiet on this Thursday, does get used—it is not just “a museum of history,” as Gus Hall used to call it. The Young Communist League meets there; some tenant groups from local public housing units will gather there as well. The party continues to publish People’s World, the publication once (and perhaps better) known as the Daily Worker. Is the publication funded primarily by rents raised from the office building? I asked. “You keep coming back to the building,” Sims said. “No.” It’s true—I kept coming back to the building. I couldn’t help it. A website mocked up by commercial real estate brokerage RDE Advisors celebrates its many advantages. “12’ ceilings throughout, with a spectacular 16’ ceiling penthouse on the 8th floor,” it reads. “What could 235 W. 23rd become?” asks another part of the site. “AI Tech Headquarters” and “Fashion Agency” are among the suggestions. The building boasts 55,362 square feet of rentable space, roughly 6,766 per floor. The site claims that all of the floors are vacant/available or soon will be, including the Communist Party’s seventh, which is not correct. The asking price—$39 per square foot, per year—looked like a bargain when compared cursorily to other available office space in the neighborhood listed online. “This is one of the few remaining opportunities in Manhattan to consolidate your entire organization under one roof,” the site says. “The landlord will consider leasing floors individually.” Crawford, the assistant, clarified to me that they would rent to most people, “though we wouldn’t rent to Turning Point USA.” The leasing agents didn’t respond to my requests for comment. I reached out to all of the tenants as well, to ask what it was like to have the Communist Party as a landlord. Only Ross Cameron, co-founder of Lyric Capital Group, a private equity firm focused on music royalties, housed on four, responded. “This email made me laugh out loud! Didn’t know there was a connection with the owner to the Communist Party and honestly didn’t know there was still an American Communist Party!” he said.
